Executive summary
Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                                                    DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



A record 1.57 million international passengers arrived at Cape Town International in
FY25/26, against 1.16 million in FY19/20, before the pandemic.1 Its average load factor of
79% in 2025 was the joint highest of South Africa's five largest airports, with Lanseria. 2
Between 2015 and 2025 the airport added 17 mainline international routes, more than any of
eight peer airports.3 On the airport count, the recovery is complete. The next stage is harder,
because the system that delivered it is now full at the peak.

Interest is not what holds the province back. About 97 million high-value travellers in 34 markets are
interested in South Africa, yet only about one in seventy of them, some 1.4 million, turns that interest into
a trip.4 Seats and bookings stand in the way. Eleven of 27 international routes run above 80% load across
the year, and at the summer peak one runway caps movements at about 30 an hour.5 In winter, carriers
pull seats: June holds 6.4% of the year's international capacity, against 10.8% in December. 6 Beyond the
Metro, most rural properties are missing from the online travel agents where visitors book. 7

On the growth model's count, arrivals can grow from about 1.5 million in 2025 to 2.5 million by 2035, if the
province wins the seats.8 That makes this strategy a supply programme before it is a marketing
programme.


The province earns a premium from a narrow base

The Western Cape earns more per visitor than the rest of South Africa. The province takes about a
quarter of national foreign spend from about a seventh of the arrivals. Its foreign visitors spend roughly 1.6
times the national average.9 That is about US$890 per arrival, against US$500 for the rest of South
Africa.10 Foreign direct spend rose 14% in a year, from R22.7bn in FY2024 to R25.9bn in FY2025. 11

Yet the premium rests on a few markets, six months and one metro. Five markets supply 63% of overseas
arrivals, and the UK, Germany and the USA alone 52%.12 More than half the arrivals on the ten largest
routes come through someone else's hub: the routes through Dubai, Doha, Addis Ababa and Istanbul
carry 53% of them.13 October to March carries between 61% and 62% of overseas visitor travel.14 The
Cape Metro holds 20,281 of the province's 50,332 tourism assets, or 40.3%, and visitors disperse from
there.15 Growth now needs new markets, new seasons and new regions.


The limits are physical and institutional

Five constraints cap the case, and none of them is a lack of interest in the province. [Summary of
Findings, 14 Oct 2026, slides 3 and 4: align these five constraints with the deck's own list once the deck is
in hand.]

African Tourism's provincial count the province received 1,538,142 in 2025, still below the 2 million of 2019, and the two series count different

Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory                                                                                           11
Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                                             DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



The gateway. The growth model draws air access as a line at 1.8 million inbound seats, and the base
path crosses it around 2030.16 The line is the model's assumption, not a measured cap. In practice the
runway binds first, at peak hours. Runway relief comes from about 2033.17 Airports Company South Africa
(ACSA) now schedules the realigned runway for December 2029, subject to contract award.18 This
strategy plans on about 2033 and counts the earlier date as upside. George Airport is domestic only, and
Cape Winelands Airport targets 2028 with no named carrier.19

Full routes. Five of the eleven full routes are mature long-haul services, led by Newark at 88.6%, Atlanta
at 84.3% and Frankfurt and London Heathrow at 83.0%.20 On full routes, carriers are expected to raise
fares before they add seats.21

Seasons. The green season, April to September, is a seat problem and a demand problem at once: seats
and visitors fall away together. Connecting traffic is "especially critical in the low season". 22 That ties winter
seats to the hub carriers' own priorities.

Reach. Accessibility falls from 4.7 out of 5 in the Cape Metro to 1.0 in the Central Karoo, and one touring
route of six is rated Ready. Beyond the Metro and the Garden Route the province is effectively car-only. 23

Levers held elsewhere. National government and its state-owned entities hold visas, air service
agreements and the airport and port programmes.24 Mobility mandates are split across three tiers, and no
approved method exists for provincial tourism GDP and jobs.25

Most of the five sit outside a marketing budget, and the runway and air rights sit outside the province.


What 2.5 million arrivals would take

Wesgro's original ask was larger: a strategy to "double international tourist arrivals to the Western Cape
by 2035".26 The evidence reframes that ambition as growth to 2.5 million. The province's Growth for Jobs
Strategy sets 3 million by 2035 on South African Tourism's count.27 That sits above every path the growth
model draws, and section 1.1 explains why the two cannot yet be compared directly. [G4J 2023 document
to be read and the 3 million target confirmed against it.]

The gateway sets the pace in the summer peak, and demand and conversion set it in the green season
and beyond the Metro. The growth model draws three paths from a 2025 base of about 1.5 million. 28 By
2035 the conservative path reaches about 1.8 million, the base path about 2.2 million and the accelerated
path about 2.5 million. Chapters 1 to 5 call the last two the base and accelerated cases.

The paths differ by intervention, not by demand potential. The base path assumes Wesgro holds its
current effort while Emirates, Qatar, Ethiopian and Turkish Airlines keep adding capacity. The accelerated
path also needs direct India and China routes and dedicated activation in India, China, the USA and the
Gulf. Without confirmed routes, it reverts to base.29

Figure ES.1. Arrivals paths to 2035
Source: Market Assessment Report, May 2026, p19 (2025 base); Demand-Side Analysis, Jun 2026, p20, p21. The 2031 base value and the
accelerated and conservative milestones are computed (section 5.3).

On the eleven full routes, marketing cannot make up for missing seats, but on the hub routes, at 65% to
72% load, it can.30 The hub markets supply about 30% of the arrivals the base path adds, the largest
share of any market group.31

The base path implies growth of 3.8% a year and the accelerated path 5.3%, against a global norm of 3%
to 5%.32 Faster growth has been sustained without a recovery tailwind. Valencia ended 2024 24.4% above
2019, and Santiago's airport grew from about 16 million passengers in 2010 to more than 26 million in
2019.33 The fastest growers aimed institutions, marketing and air access at one binding constraint.34 For
Chile that constraint was long-haul access. The Western Cape's constraint has the same shape: seats into
one gateway airport.


The strategy makes three shifts

In Pillar 1, Reshape demand, the work moves from awareness to conversion. The province is under-sold
in its growth markets and hard to book outside its core. Three of its six focus areas aim at conversion:
green-season events, bookable product and hub traffic already in the air. About 101,000 Indian and
Chinese travellers a year already reach Cape Town, mainly through Gulf and African hubs.35 Singapore
shows how a hub can turn transit into visits, and Melbourne uses its events calendar to steady demand. 36

In Pillar 2, Transform supply, the work moves from new attractions to seats and finished experiences. The
pillar has one capacity entry: frequency and larger aircraft on the full routes ahead of runway relief.
Following Chile, Initiative I6 designs a public-private air access fund in 2027 and 2028 to back it (section

Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory                                                                             13
Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                     DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



4.3).37 Five more cover cruise, reach, product and experience. Cruise grows through ships and excursions
rather than port capital.38 At the flagship sites the task is to manage flows, because Boulders is near
carrying capacity and Cableway queues run up to three hours.39 Elsewhere it is to turn existing assets into
complete visitor experiences.40

In Pillar 3, Build enabling capability, the work moves from separate mandates to shared decisions, capital
and data. The pillar answers four questions. First, who benefits: twenty township and rural communities
hold 18.4% of the province's residents, but the spend traced to them is a floor of 0.02% to 0.04% of
foreign direct spend.41 Second, who pays: the capital pipeline funds airports, ports, roads and rail, but
almost none of it reaches the beds, booking and product the regions lack.42 Third, who decides: with visas
and air service agreements held nationally, the province leads by escalation.43 Fourth, who measures:
more than twenty bodies produce tourism data, yet no one holds a single view of visitor behaviour. 44 The
New Zealand case argues for yield over arrivals, so the plan tracks spend, season and spread beside
arrivals (section 5.3).45

The three pillars make one case. Demand work fills seats only if the seats exist, and supply work pays
only if the product can be booked. Both need the enabling base to last.

Twenty focus areas at a glance

The strategy rests on a hypothesis tree of three pillars, six themes and twenty focus areas (section 1.4).
Each focus area names a lever on growth, framed so the evidence could test it. Nineteen were tested
against the evidence of a named workstream. The evidence supports all nineteen, nine strongly and ten
with a gap or a qualifier (Appendix A1). The twentieth, workforce and service skills, waits on a labour
analysis still to be commissioned.

Source: Letsema; Appendix A1 traces each focus area to its evidence.

Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                                 DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION




Seats and bookings move first

Air access and bookability move first, because every other lever runs through them. The plan runs in three
phases. Activate, from 2027 to 2028, holds and adds seats on the full routes, counts the bookability gap
and sets up the institutions the rest depends on. Gate 1 closes the phase at the end of 2028, two years
before the base path crosses the seat line.46 Expand, from 2029 to 2031, scales what works and meets
the seat line at the 2030 mid-term review. Sustain, from 2032 to 2035, takes direct route cases to carriers
as runway capacity arrives.

Eighteen initiatives carry the plan, one per focus area except where one owner delivers two. Wesgro is
accountable for twelve. The Department of Economic Development and Tourism (DEDAT), the
Department of Mobility, municipalities, local tourism organisations (LTOs) and site operators lead the other
six. Thirteen draw mainly on Wesgro's operating budget. None has yet been costed, and setting the rand
bands is the first funding task (section 4.6).

Twelve actions in three strands can start in the first 24 months, nine of them inside Wesgro's current
mandate and budget.

Seats and season come first, from the first half of 2027. Cape Town Air Access holds winter seats through
joint marketing with carriers on the full routes and tracks the runway quarterly (I6, I2). The Convention
Bureau publishes a green-season events calendar and aims bids at May to September (I2).

Governance and baselines also start then. The Wesgro CEO's office and DEDAT constitute the Tourism
Growth Steerco and table the first national escalation agenda (I15, I17). Wesgro's Research unit agrees a
data-sharing protocol with ACSA, SA Tourism, Stats SA and Cape Town Tourism (I16). It also runs a
bookability census by district and requests visitor counts from the five flagship sites (I3, I11).

Product and inclusion follow in the second half of 2027. Wesgro's Tourism unit packages the Garden
Route and Route 62 with a shuttle transfer (I8, I9). It also packages the Southern Line as a visitor product
with the City of Cape Town and the Passenger Rail Agency of South Africa (I8). It chooses two or three
pilot communities, and from the first half of 2028 the LTOs give each community one live booking route
before any promotion (I12, I3). Each action opens a seat, a baseline or a booking path a later initiative
needs. Owners and start dates are proposals for Steerco to confirm (section 4.5).


How the Board will track progress

The Board will judge the plan on seven impact indicators, and today only arrivals and spend carry a
modelled value for 2035. Beneath them sit twenty-five outcome and thirty-six output indicators. Steerco
receives a quarterly scorecard and the Board an annual outlook against both paths. Gates at the end of
2028 and 2031, a mid-term review in 2030 and a terminal evaluation in 2035 decide what each phase
funds.

The table shows the model's arrivals baseline, and the airport count is tracked beside it, from 1.44 million
in FY24/25 (section 5.3).47 The spend target needs the same care. The model's 2024 spend of R27.12bn
sits 19% above the R22.7bn reported for FY2024, so R40.6bn is not measured from R25.9bn. 48 [Open
item: Skift to rebase the spend path on the FY2025 actual of R25.9bn.]

Jobs wait on the labour analysis, so the ambition of 300,000 tourism-linked jobs is tested before it
becomes a target. The green-season share starts at 38.1%, but the share outside the Metro has no
baseline until the data protocol delivers one in 2027 (section 5.3). Gate 1 turns the missing baselines into
targets, so the first two years must produce data as well as results.


What could knock it off course

Ten of the fourteen material risks sit outside Wesgro's control, so the response to most is early warning
and a route to the tier that can act. One is rated high on both likelihood and impact: restrictive bilateral air
agreements and the US carrier cap. It calls for an escalation agenda with stated asks, plus route work
inside existing rights.

Six are rated medium likelihood and high impact, in three pairs; first, two sit with the national tier and
ACSA. Runway relief may slip past about 2033, so its milestones are tracked quarterly. Visa processing
may not improve, and the escalation agenda carries it. Second, two sit in the markets. A Gulf hub
disruption would cut connecting capacity, so hub work is spread across all four hubs. A downturn in the
UK, Germany and the USA would hit 52% of overseas arrivals, so activation shifts towards Africa and the
hub markets. Third, two sit closer to delivery. Safety incidents could deter visitors, so safety gates come
before any pilot launch. A cut to Wesgro's operating budget would hit thirteen initiatives, so rand bands
and a stop list are due before Gate 1.

Two more are rated high likelihood and medium impact. Climate events that close sites and routes call for
a climate risk register. The loss of LTO funding, which MFMA Circular No. 131 has disrupted, calls for
stable funding agreements with a development mandate. Likelihood and impact are Letsema's
assessment, and Steerco reviews the register each quarter (section 4.8).


What the Board is asked to decide

Five decisions start the plan, and two recommendations pass partners the levers Wesgro does not hold.

Chapter 1


Context
Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                                           DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION




1.1 Mandate and objective
Wesgro commissioned a strategy to "double international tourist arrivals to the Western
Cape by 2035".49 The evidence reframes that goal as growth to 2.5 million arrivals, paced by
gateway capacity in the summer peak and by demand in the green season.

Wesgro acts through partners to create jobs
Wesgro promotes tourism, trade and investment for Cape Town and the Western Cape, so the tourism
goal sits inside a wider economic brief. Its mission is to work "together with partners, drive inclusive and
sustainable economic growth, strengthen competitiveness, and create jobs in Cape Town and the
Western Cape". Its vision is to "be a leading regional tourism, trade and investment promotion agency". 50
Two points in the mission shape this strategy: growth is a means to jobs, and Wesgro acts through
partners rather than holding the levers itself.

Four of Wesgro's eight strategy statements bear directly on this study.51 The first sets the destination
proposition: Cape Town and the Western Cape "top of mind as a leading business and tourism destination
with premium products and services". The fourth names new markets and buyers in China, ASEAN, India,
Brazil and the Middle East, "while maintaining a continued focus on established markets". The sixth
commits Wesgro to "Track and address systemic challenges influencing economic competitiveness", and
the seventh to "Provide economic data, insights & analytics for the Western Cape". The strategy's growth-
market work, focus area 4, centres on two of them: India and China, whose travellers already reach Cape
Town through Gulf and African hubs.52 The growth model's acceleration group holds the Gulf, China, India
and Brazil.53

Of four ambitions, only arrivals has a target the evidence supports
The objective, set in November 2025, is "To develop an evidence-based tourism strategy that will double
international tourist arrivals to the Western Cape by 2035". It continues: "through a holistic approach that
drives demand, strengthens supply and enables inclusive, sustainable and geographically dispersed
growth aligned with WESGRO's strategic priorities".54 The objective fixes the three pillars the report keeps
throughout: demand, supply and enablement. Of its terms, only the doubling did not survive the evidence.

Four headline ambitions sat under the objective: 2.8 million international visitors, 300,000 tourism-linked
jobs, 10,000 SMMEs onboarded and twice the airlift and cruise volumes.55 The strategy carries all four
forward. The evidence reframed the first and changed how the other three are measured.

Arrivals. The growth model tested the doubling and supports 2.2 million to 2.5 million by 2035. From
1,498,829 arrivals in 2025, its base case reaches 2,175,778 by 2035 and its accelerated case 2,516,282,
above a conservative floor of 1,822,634.56 The base case holds Wesgro's effort at 2025 intensity, with
selective frequency gains and continued hub expansion. The accelerated case needs confirmed direct
routes to India and China, above-baseline activation and more hub capacity into Cape Town; without the
routes, volume reverts to base.57 Growth to 2.5 million is that case rounded, and it is the goal the report
carries.

Even the base case outruns the model's air access. The model draws a seat line at 1.8 million inbound
seats, and the base case first passes it at the 2030 year point, at 1,802,929 arrivals. 58 The line is an
assumption rather than a measured cap. In practice the runway binds first, at peak hours (section 1.3).
Growth to 2.5 million is therefore a supply programme before it is a marketing programme.

Jobs. The ambition was 300,000 tourism-linked jobs, yet the evidence holds no Western Cape tourism
employment figure (section 2.4). The strategy takes jobs into Chapter 5 without a baseline. Wesgro and
DEDAT should commission a labour analysis in 2027 to set one (focus area 20).

SMMEs. The ambition was 10,000 SMMEs onboarded, with no baseline or definition printed [Wesgro to
confirm the baseline count and definition of SMMEs onboarded]. The strategy treats it as community
inclusion, measured in visitor spend reaching priority communities rather than a count of firms (section 2.4
and Chapter 5).

Airlift and cruise. The ambition was twice the airlift and cruise volumes. Section 3.2.1 sets airlift as the
seats and routes needed to lift the model's 1.8 million seat line.59 It sets cruise as calls and ports [Cruise
Cape Town to confirm the call target and its definition]. The cruise baseline is 79 vessels in the 2024/25
season.60 Of the four ambitions, only arrivals has a target the evidence supports today. The other three go
forward with their baselines still to be fixed.

Wesgro's arrivals metric fits a ten-year reading
Wesgro measures itself against six strategic outcomes, each with one metric. Two bear on tourism:
800,000 to 1 million new international tourist arrivals and 161 business event bids secured, worth an
estimated R4.5 billion.61 None of the six states a period [Wesgro to confirm the period over which the six
outcome metrics are measured].

The period decides how the arrivals metric compares with the growth case. Over ten years, 800,000 to 1
million new arrivals sits between the two paths, which add 676,949 and 1,017,453 from 2025 to 2035. 62
Yet over the five years to 2030 it is more than twice what the base case adds by then, about 304,000. 63

The business-events metric ties to the strategy's green-season focus. International association meetings
in Cape Town rose from 33 in 2022 to 58 in 2024 and are "primarily hosted between May and
November".64 That window overlaps the green season, April to September,65 but 74 of the 136 meetings
from 2022 to 2024 fell between September and November.66 Bids must therefore pull meetings into the
trough months (section 3.1). Chapter 5 tracks the nearest indicators: international arrivals and association
meetings in Cape Town (T1-01 and T2-02).

The Growth for Jobs target sits above both paths
The study's contract requires this report and its recommendations to align with two documents: the Growth
for Jobs Strategy (G4J) and Wesgro's FY26 to FY30 plan.67 This report is the Final Research Report
under clause 1.1.14, and Chapter 5 answers the monitoring and evaluation clauses 1.1.4 and 1.1.10
[clause references to be checked against the signed SLA Annexure B, whose wording is not in hand].

The Growth for Jobs Strategy 2023, approved by Cabinet, designates tourism a primary tradable sector
for the province.68 Its arrivals target is 3 million international tourist arrivals by 2035 on the South African
Tourism basis.69 [G4J 2023 document to be read and the 3 million target confirmed against it.] On that
basis the province received 1,538,142 in 2025,70 still below the 2 million of 2019 on which G4J builds. The
3 million sits above both of the model's paths. Part of the gap is method: G4J adds 1 million to the 2019
count, while the model builds up market by market against the seats the gateway can carry. Part may be
measure, because what the model counts is not yet confirmed (see the closing note). Until it is, the 2.5
million cannot be set directly against the 3 million.

Wesgro's FY26 to FY30 plan is the second test. Wesgro's strategy statements are quoted above at
second hand, but the FY26 to FY30 plan itself, with its tourism targets, is not in the evidence base. 71 Until
it is, alignment can be stated only in principle [Wesgro to supply the FY26 to FY30 plan and its tourism
targets, so that the Chapter 5 baselines and the Chapter 4 phase gates can be mapped to the plan cycle].
Yet the timing already fits. Chapter 4 sets its first decision gate at the end of 2028, inside the plan period
and before the base case crosses the model's seat line.

How the report counts arrivals
Four kinds of arrivals figure circulate around this strategy: ambitions, airport passengers, South African
Tourism's tourist arrivals and the model's arrivals. Appendix A2 lists all eight with their bases. The report
keeps them apart under three rules. Airport passenger arrivals at Cape Town International, by fiscal year,
are the baseline and tracking series, with FY24/25 at 1.44 million.72 The model's 2025 base of 1,498,829
and its 2035 points are the target series. [Open item: Skift to confirm in writing what the model counts (SA
Tourism tourist arrivals, ACSA passengers including returning residents or OAG origin-and-destination
flows) and how the Hubs group is built.] South African Tourism's provincial count is used once, to read
across to the G4J target. The report quotes the series side by side and never adds one to the other [model
owner to confirm the series and year basis behind the 2025 base of 1,498,829].


1.2 The Western Cape tourism system today

The Western Cape's international tourism has recovered to a record, and the system that
delivered the recovery runs through five source markets, six summer months and one metro.

Arrivals and recovery
International passenger arrivals at Cape Town International reached 1.44m in FY24/25, against 1.16m in
FY19/20, the last pre-pandemic year.73 Arrivals passed the pre-pandemic peak of 1.19m, set in FY18/19,
during FY23/24, when they reached 1.36m.74 FY25/26 added a further step to 1.57m.75

The fall and the climb were both steep. Arrivals dropped to 80,400 in FY20/21 and 378,500 in FY21/22,
then returned to 1.07m in FY22/23.76 Two-way international passengers reached 2.90m in FY24/25, 29%
above FY17/18,77 and 3.3m in calendar 2025 inside a total of 11.1m, both up 7% on the year.78

Spend and value
Foreign direct spend in the province rose from R22.7bn in FY2024 to R25.9bn in FY2025, a gain of 14% in
one year.84 The Western Cape earns that spend from a modest share of the national market. Across the
four quarters of 2025 it took 16%, 13%, 13% and 16% of South Africa's foreign arrivals and 26%, 20%,
22% and 27% of foreign direct spend.85

The gap between the two shares is the province's premium. About a quarter of national spend from about
a seventh of arrivals means the average foreign visitor to the Western Cape spends roughly 1.6 times the

national average.86 The province earns about US$890 per international arrival, against US$500 for the rest
of South Africa and US$630 for Morocco, though below Kenya's US$1,460.87

More than a quarter of overseas visitors stay between 7 and 21 days, on printed bands that sum to only
48%.88 Some 5% stay 1 to 2 days, 12% 3 to 4, 14% 7 to 10, 3% 11 to 14, 13% 15 to 21 and 1% other. 89
The Diagnostic prints the same distribution from the 2024 Wesgro Visitor Tracking Survey, in which
52.33% of respondents gave no length of stay, so the bands describe about half the sample. 90

The modal visitor in the 2024 Wesgro survey spends R1,000 to R2,000 a day, is aged 36 to 50 and travels
as a pair.91 Nearly 40% spend more than R1,000 a day.92 Self-catering at 17.4% and guesthouses at 9.7%
lead accommodation choice, ahead of hotels at 3.6%;93 together these shares cover under a third of
respondents.

Source markets
Five markets supply 63% of overseas arrivals. In 2025 the UK held 19%, Germany 17%, the USA 16%,
the Netherlands 7% and France 4%, and the three largest alone held 52%.94 The report uses 63%, the
sum of the printed shares.

Concentration is not new. On the Cape Town overseas air-arrivals basis for January to August 2024 the
same five markets held 61%, with the UK and the USA at 18% each and Germany at 15%. 95 The growth
markets remain small: India supplied about 2.6% of South Africa's overseas tourists in September 2025,
the Middle East 2.3% and China 1.4%.96

The mature markets also pay the most. Average spend per visitor in 2024 ran from R32,900 for the USA,
R26,500 for the UK and R25,600 for Germany down to R8,600 for Namibia and R8,500 for Zimbabwe. 97
The spread is 3.9 times, and the two regional markets compensate with stability, repeat demand and
counter-seasonal volume.98

Most of these visitors arrive through someone else's hub. The UAE route carried 222,923 air arrivals into
Cape Town in 2025, the UK 158,448, Qatar 126,078, Ethiopia 123,250 and Turkiye 89,090. 99 The four hub
routes through Dubai, Doha, Addis Ababa and Istanbul carried 53% of arrivals on the ten largest routes, 100
so the hubs, not the source markets, are the province's working gateways.

Air and sea access
Ten airlines fly to Cape Town International from Africa, nine from Europe, three from North and South
America, two from the Middle East and one from Asia.106 The aviation assessment states the gap plainly
as "the absence of any direct capacity to any of the Asian markets", India and China included. 107 Twenty-
nine international routes serve the airport in 2026.108

Cape Town International itself is now the physical ceiling. One runway with no parallel taxiway caps
movements at about 30 an hour, and ACSA's programme of about R11.3bn brings relief from about 2032
for the international terminal and about 2033 for the runway.109 ACSA's latest schedule sets December
2026 to December 2029 for the realigned runway, subject to contract award.110 The secondary gateways
do not change the picture: George, above its design limit at 911,000 passengers in 2025, doubles by 2028
or 2029, with construction from October 2026,111 and Cape Winelands Airport targets 2028 with no named
carrier.112

Cruise has grown without new berth capacity. The province hosted 70, 67 and 79 vessels in the 2022/23,
2023/24 and 2024/25 seasons, carrying 144,125, 109,884 and 127,178 passengers.113 The Infrastructure
Report counts 83 calls at the Cape Town berth in 2024/25, 127,000 passengers and R1.79bn of economic
impact.114 [StratEcon 2024/25 cruise report to confirm whether 79 vessels and 83 calls differ by definition.]

Port reliability, not berth space, is the risk. The single berth ran at about 40% of a season capacity of 150
or more calls, and its limits are a 300m ship length and the South-Easter wind.115 The Diagnostic calls the
Port of Cape Town "a binding constraint, with congestion and equipment failures affecting export and
cruise reliability".116

Geography of the system
The Cape Metro generates 72.5% of provincial GDPR, a whole-economy share rather than a tourism
one.117 The Cape Winelands follows at 11.2%, the Garden Route at 7.6%, the West Coast at 4.8%, the
Overberg at 3% and the Central Karoo at 0.5%.118 The decks date only the Garden Route share, to 2023,
while the Metro chart is titled 2024 and the rest carry no year.119

Tourism supply is concentrated in the same way. The Infrastructure Report mapped 50,332 tourism assets
across the six regions, of which the Metro holds 20,281, or 40.3%.120 The Garden Route holds 9,875, the
Cape Winelands 7,730, the Overberg 6,176, the West Coast 4,752 and the Central Karoo 1,518. 121

Cape Town is the axle of the system, "the province's global shopfront and primary dispersal hub" in the
Infrastructure Report's words.122 The Diagnostic warns that "any capacity failure in Cape Town quickly
becomes a provincial risk, not just a city problem".123 International visitors land there and disperse from
there, and readiness falls with each step away: accessibility scores drop from 4.7 in the Metro to 1.0 in the
Central Karoo.124

Beyond the Metro the visitor drives. The N2 is "the only continuous inter-regional tourism corridor, creating
a single point of failure", and large parts of the province outside the Metro and the Garden Route remain
effectively car-only destinations.125 The further from Cape Town, the faster the infrastructure falls away.126

Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                                               DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



Rail, roads and utilities
Passenger rail has collapsed as a mode. Trainsets fell from about 110 in 1995 to about 91, pushing some
400,000 commuters onto saturated roads, and congestion in the Metro costs an estimated R2.8bn a
year.127 Metrorail still runs 460km, five lines and 104 of 121 stations, with 22.7m passenger trips in
FY2025, but no line reaches the airport and it is rated functionally unusable for tourism. 128

Utilities cap the periphery before demand does. Water scarcity in high-growth nodes such as the Garden
Route acts as a "hard ceiling" for visitor load, and load shedding raises costs and degrades the
experience in peripheral districts.129 The airport has no fuel pipeline and depends on road tankers,130 and
the 2017 to 2018 water crisis left a lasting perception of resource pressure.131

The remedies are named but not scheduled. The Diagnostic lists four transport enablers, none dated or
costed: the Cape Town Integrator, rail restoration and devolution, a high-speed city to airport link and
George to Cape Winelands passenger rail.132 PRASA's R21.1bn programme and the City's R123bn, 30-
year devolution case cover fleet and lines, and neither carries a tourism product or an airport link. 133

Institutions
Wesgro runs the demand side of this system through six units: Cape Town Air Access, Cruise Cape
Town, the Convention Bureau, Destination Marketing, Research and Tourism.134 Around them sit fifteen
external organisations, from national agencies and state-owned entities to the province, the City,
destination and attraction bodies and private operators.135

The levers that bind the system sit at different tiers. National government holds visa rules, border
compliance, air service agreements and route rights.136 The province holds jobs, inclusion and spatial
outcomes, sector development and infrastructure planning; local government holds service delivery,
precinct management and events readiness; industry holds route viability, demand generation and product
standards.137

Alignment across those tiers is thin. Stakeholders described strain at SA Tourism and a lack of fiscal
space.138 More than twenty bodies produce tourism data and "there is no single view of visitor behaviour,
making strategy reactive rather than predictive".139

Stakeholders say the same. Fifteen of the sixteen organisations interviewed raised industry and
government collaboration.140 The Department of Economic Development named restrictive bilateral air
agreements and destination funding materially below peer destinations as high-impact constraints. 141

Wesgro's six units sit inside a system of fifteen external organisations whose decision rights are split
across three spheres of government and industry.142 The six units are Cape Town Air Access, Cruise
Cape Town, the Convention Bureau, Destination Marketing, Research and Tourism. Between them they
cover route development, cruise, business events bids, destination marketing, research and tourism
promotion for Cape Town and the Western Cape.

Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                               DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



The Western Cape Government (Economic Development and Tourism) and the City of Cape Town make
up provincial and city government. The provincial tier sets jobs, inclusion and spatial outcomes, runs
sector development and safety programmes and plans infrastructure and resilience. The local tier delivers
services and manages precincts. It also readies events and place experience and handles congestion, by-
laws and community impact.

South African Tourism, Airports Company South Africa, Transnet and the South African Maritime Safety
Authority sit at the national tier. It holds visa rules and border compliance. It also holds air service
agreements and route rights, sets national brand and investment priorities and runs airport capacity and
port operations.

Cape Town Tourism, the V&A Waterfront, CapeNature, South African National Parks and the Table
Mountain Aerial Cableway are the destination and attraction bodies. They carry demand generation and
destination marketing as well as product quality and visitor experience at the flagship sites. The private
operators, MSC Cruises, Cape Grace, Pullman Hotels and Resorts (Cape Town City Centre) and
Southern Sun, carry route and product viability and commercial performance. They also set pricing and
service standards. Together they form the industry tier.

None of these constraints is a shortage of visitors. The runway, the single corridor, a rail network visitors
cannot use, the thin winter schedule and decision rights split across three tiers all sit outside a marketing
budget. The system has recovered; its constraints are now physical and institutional.


1.3 From recovery to growth

The recovery is complete; gateway capacity caps growth in the summer peak, and demand
and conversion cap it in the green season.

The limits of recovery logic
The strategy's task is to move the Western Cape from recovery to growth. The first half is done.
International passenger arrivals at Cape Town International reached 1.44m in FY24/25 against 1.16m in
FY19/20, and 1.57m in FY25/26.143

Recovery logic assumed capacity waiting to be refilled. Arrivals fell to 80,400 in FY20/21,144 and the task
was to refill idle seats, rooms and routes. That slack is gone. South Africa's international seat capacity is
back at pre-pandemic levels, and Cape Town International shares the highest average load factor of the
country's five largest airports with Lanseria, 79% in 2025.145

Cape Town did more than refill its seats. It added 17 mainline international routes between 2015 and
2025, more than any of eight peer airports, and MIDAS calls the result "a 'what next' moment". 146 The
rebound that a recovery plan is built to capture has already happened.

Seats in summer, demand in winter
Growth is now supply-constrained in the summer peak and demand-constrained in the green season,
when airlines withdraw seats: June carries 6.4% of the year's international capacity.147 Skift's survey of 34
outbound markets counts 97 million high-value, South Africa-interested travellers, of whom only 1.4 million



143
    Demand-Side Analysis, Jun 2026, p11.

convert into arrivals.148 The report's conclusion is that "the challenge is not demand generation but
improving conversion into actual visits".149

The binding supply is peak capacity at the gateway. The growth model draws current air access as a line
at 1.8 million inbound seats, and the base case, in which Wesgro maintains current activity, reaches
1,802,929 arrivals at the 2030 plotted year.150 The chart below sets the base case against that line.




Figure 1.4. Base case against the seat line
Source: Market Assessment Report, May 2026, p20 (base case by year from a 2025 base of 1,498,829; the 2028 to 2035 points are identical
in the Demand-Side Analysis, Jun 2026, p21). Seat line as drawn on the same slide. Airport relief from about 2032: Infrastructure Report, Jul
2026, p103. The model prints only these six year points and no seat table beneath the 1.8m line, so the crossing falls between 2028
(1,673,855) and 2030 (1,802,929). The conservative (1,822,634) and accelerated (2,516,282) cases are printed as 2035 end points only and
are not plotted.

The line is the model's assumption, not a measured cap. At Cape Town International's 79% average load
factor, 1.8 million inbound seats would carry about 1.42 million arrivals, fewer than the 1.57 million ACSA
recorded in FY25/26.151 MIDAS tables 3,816,797 international seats for the year to November 2025, more
than 1.8 million each way even if that total counts both directions.152 What binds is the runway at peak
hours in the summer peak, at about 30 movements an hour.153 [Open item: ACSA or OAG to supply peak-
hour and seasonal slot headroom at Cape Town International.]

The routes that exist are full. Eleven of the 27 international routes in the load-factor table ran above 80%
in the year to November 2025, and the network averaged 76.0% on the printed total. 154 MIDAS tables 27
routes for the year to November 2025; 29 serve the airport in 2026. Five of the eleven are mature long-
haul routes, led by Newark at 88.6%, Atlanta at 84.3% and Frankfurt and London Heathrow at 83.0%. 155

Full routes do not add seats of their own accord. MIDAS expects that "rather than increase capacity
carriers will seek to increase revenues via higher selling fares rather than add new services".156 Airlines

also pull seats out of winter: June carries 6.4% of the year's international capacity and December
10.8%.157

Behind the routes sits the runway. "The airport, not a shortage of visitors is what now caps Cape Town's
tourism growth".158 One runway with no parallel taxiway caps movements at about 30 an hour, and
ACSA's programme of about R11.3bn brings relief from about 2032 for the terminal and about 2033 for the
runway.159

The alternatives do not close the gap in time. George, domestic only, doubles by 2028 or 2029, and Cape
Winelands Airport targets 2028 with no named carrier. In the Infrastructure Report's words, "the certain fix
is the latest, and the earliest big-capacity option is the least certain".160 Route rights sit a tier up: air service
agreements are a national function.161 The Department of Economic Development rates restrictive bilateral
agreements a high-impact constraint on airlift.162

Car-only regions and unbookable product
Beyond the Metro the constraint changes shape, not nature. Accessibility scores fall from 4.7 in the Cape
Metro to 1.0 in the Central Karoo.163 Large parts of the province outside the Metro and the Garden Route
remain effectively car-only destinations.164 The further from Cape Town, the faster the infrastructure falls
away.165

Much of what the car can reach cannot be booked. Most rural properties have no OTA presence and
Route 62 has the lowest online bookability of the six touring routes.166 Even CapeNature takes about 70%
of its reservations through a call centre rather than its online platform.167

Growth above the norm
Global tourism grows 3 to 5% a year under stable conditions, the band the Benchmarking Report calls
organic growth.168 Five of the six comparator destinations grew faster.169 Chile grew 40.4%, Singapore
21%, Kenya 14.6%, Valencia 13 to 14% and New Zealand about 12%, against global growth of 11% in
2024.170 New Zealand's figure matches its 2025 holiday-arrivals growth rather than the 5.9% for total
arrivals in 2025.171 Melbourne's 4.3% is an Australia-wide forecast rather than an actual,172 so the
comparator range runs from about 4% to 40%.

The Western Cape's two paths straddle the band. The base case implies 3.8% a year from 2025 to 2035
and the accelerated case 5.3%, and the accelerated case reverts to base "without confirmed routes". 173
The Benchmarking Report counts growth as structural only above that band. It must also be "driven by
deliberate changes to demand drivers, access conditions or product systems, rather than cyclical recovery
or short-term promotion".174

Cape Town is the axle of the system: international visitors arrive there and disperse from there, and "Cape
Town remains the engine of the system, but growth cannot keep concentrating there".175 The Department
of Economic Development already reads the growth framing as overweight on volumes and asks for a
value and spend lens in place of arrivals.176 Growth now needs new markets, new seasons and new
regions, and each of those is a supply problem before it is a marketing problem.


1.4 Approach and method

The study ran in four phases from November 2025, tested one hypothesis tree against every
interim deliverable and took each figure from a ranked hierarchy of sources.

Four phases
The Inception Report set out four phases with printed durations: inception, one month; demand-side
analysis, four months; supply-side analysis, three months; enablement and final reporting, seven
months.177 The Phase 4 detail slide prints six months for the same phase;178 the report uses seven, which
matches the Phase 4 bar on the deliverables gantt, May to the end of November. 179 The deliverables gantt
runs November to November without printing years.180

Each phase had a distinct job.181 Inception aligned expectations and ways of working and produced the
Current State Diagnostic as a literature-based baseline.182 The demand phase covered source markets,
India and China route to market, benchmarks and trends; the supply phase covered spatial readiness and
community inclusion, and the two overlapped from February to April.183

The final phase built the M&E framework, consolidated the strategy into this report and prepared the
launch.184 Three mechanisms governed the work: fortnightly working sessions with Wesgro, Steering
Committee sessions at milestones and fortnightly written status updates.185 The session calendar ran from
the kick-off on 13 November 2025 to a final-report Steerco pencilled for 12 June 2026.186

The hypothesis tree
The analytical core is a hypothesis tree, in which each entry names a lever on growth and is framed so the
evidence could test it. Three pillars carry the tree: Reshape demand, Transform supply and Build enabling
capability. Each pillar holds two themes, and the six themes hold twenty focus areas [Summary of
Findings, 14 Oct 2026, pp15 to 16: deck not in hand; page citation to confirm]. Nineteen of the twenty
were tested against the evidence of a named workstream, and Appendix A1 traces each to the evidence
behind it.

The tree disciplined the evidence. The stakeholder work coded each of its 223 insight statements to a
pillar and a category of the tree before aggregating them.187 Because the study team did the coding, the
counts show where views cluster; they do not on their own confirm a focus area. Each later deliverable
marked the branch of the tree it addressed.

Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                        DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



The workstreams divide the tree between them. The demand and aviation work tested five of the six
demand focus areas and the case for routes and seats. The infrastructure work tested cruise, mobility,
product, visitor experience and capital. The inclusion work tested community inclusion and, with the
stakeholder and infrastructure work, bookability. The stakeholder engagement supplied most of the
evidence on climate risk, the flagship nature sites and the governance theme. The twentieth focus area,
workforce and service skills, has no workstream yet. The 300,000-jobs ambition has no labour analysis
behind it.188 Section 1.1 asks Wesgro and DEDAT to commission one in 2027.

Source hierarchy
Every figure in this report carries a source and page, and sources rank in five tiers. Where tiers disagree
the higher tier wins; where two documents in one tier disagree, both values are recorded and the value
used is stated with the reason. Nothing is reconciled by averaging.

      1. Interim deliverables, cited by the date printed on the cover and by PDF page index, since none
         prints page numbers.
      2. Working analyses: the Market Assessment Report of May 2026 and the three Skift memos on digital
         and search insights (December 2025), segmentation (March 2026) and African markets (March
         2026), cited by section.
      3. Wesgro and government data cited through a deliverable:189 the Mero 2024 reports for all six
         districts, the Pero 2025 and the Growth for Jobs Strategy 2023. The same tier holds the Tourism
         Blueprint, Wesgro's five-year strategy and the StratEcon cruise liner economic contribution studies
         for 2023, 2024 and 2024/25.
      4. Benchmark and reference reports, such as the UN Tourism barometer behind the global growth
         norm.190
      5. External public sources, used only where no higher tier carries the figure and labelled as such.
Two conventions follow. The MIDAS Aviation Market Assessment and the Skift memos are Word files
without page numbers, so they are cited by section title. The summary deck of October 2026 ranks below
the deliverables: where the two differ, the deliverable's figure is used and the difference is logged.

Interim deliverables
Nine dated documents carry the evidence. Appendix A9 dates each by its cover and gives the version in
hand and the SLA clauses it answers. Chapter 2 draws them together as one body of analysis in four
parts: the global benchmarks and their lessons (2.1), demand (2.2), supply (2.3) and the enabling
environment (2.4). The Inception Report frames 1.1 and 1.4, and the Current State Diagnostic frames 1.2.
The Market Assessment Report of May 2026 is the working analysis behind the Demand-Side Analysis
and alone prints the 2025 base the growth case uses.191

Stakeholder engagement
Stakeholder engagement ran in four phases alongside the analysis.192 Introduction and framing ran from
January to March 2026, sense-making and validation in April and May, strategy socialisation in June and
July and mobilisation from August into October. The Stakeholder Insights Report closed the first phase
with insight tables for sixteen organisations, from Mossel Bay to the CTICC. 193 Sixteen is a count of those
tables; the report prints no tally.

The method was strict about what an insight is. Sessions were run, insights extracted, each coded to a
pillar and a category of the tree and then aggregated.194 The 223 statements are the project team's
paraphrases rated High, Medium or Low, not quotations, so this report attributes them to organisations
and never to named individuals.195

Two gaps in the record matter: phase 1 engaged no airline, online travel agent or tour operator, and the
six demand-side engagements were proposed for phase 2.196 [Open item: test the gateway and
governance chapters with ACSA, the Cape Town Air Access airline partners, the City of Cape Town and
Cape Town Tourism before Steerco.] The demand work drew instead on discussions with more than 20
stakeholders of its own.197 The outcomes of engagement phases 2 to 4 sit in no document in hand
[Letsema to supply the phase 2 to 4 engagement record for Appendix A6].

Data limits
Five limits in the data shape how the findings read. The headline arrivals series counts international
passenger arrivals at Cape Town International, not tourists, and Cape Town air arrivals stand proxy for the
province in the market shares.198 No approved methodology exists for provincial tourism GDP and jobs,
and no deliverable prints a Western Cape tourism employment baseline.199

The supply dataset behind the Infrastructure Report (Supply_Infrastructure.xlsx) holds no geography
below district, and its 17,195 records added after May 2026 are generated placeholders, as the study
team found when it checked the file. [Open item: confirm whether the Infrastructure Report's count of
50,332 assets includes those records; if it does, restate Tables 1.2, 2.4 and A.18 and the district shares
drawn from them.] The inclusion analysis runs on an OpenStreetMap asset layer, extracted on 16
September 2026, joined to Census 2011 sub-places, so its resident shares are fifteen years old. 200

The growth model exists in two editions. The May Market Assessment runs from a 2025 base of
1,498,829 arrivals and the June Demand-Side Analysis from a 2024 base of 1,445,031.201 Both reach
2,175,778 in the base case and 2,516,282 in the accelerated case by 2035. The report runs the growth
case from the 2025 base, keeps FY24/25's 1.44m as the recovery baseline and states which is which
wherever both appear.

Completion plan
A six-week completion plan agreed on 12 October 2026 governs the last stage of the work. [Summary of
Findings, 14 Oct 2026: six-week plan from 12 October, with weekly outputs and owners, to be inserted;
deck not in hand.] It replaces the inception timetable, which placed report consolidation between 1 June
and 27 July 2026 and launch support to 2 November.202 The report goes to Steerco, Exco and the Board
before the public launch in February 2027.


1.5 How to read this report

The report is built so that a Board member can read the summary and any one focus area
on its own, and a programme owner can run the plan from Chapters 3 to 5.

Chapter map
The report moves from context to evidence to recommendations to delivery.

The executive summary tells the case in short: where the province stands, what caps growth and what 2.5
million arrivals would take. It then sets out the three shifts and the twenty focus areas, the first 24 months,
the headline indicators, the main risks and the Board's decisions. It stands alone, for the Board, Exco and
the February 2027 launch audience. Chapter 1 is for every reader, and is the chapter of the body a Board
member should read if they read no other. It sets out what Wesgro asked for, where the system stands
after recovery, why the next step is constrained by peak capacity and how the study ran.

Chapter 2 sets out what the analysis found, in four parts: the global benchmarks and their lessons,
demand, supply and the enabling environment. It serves readers checking the evidence behind a Chapter
3 entry, and each part closes with a box naming the focus areas it supports. Chapter 3 takes the twenty
focus areas in turn. Each entry sets out the challenge or opportunity, the evidence, the course of action,
why it can work and the link to the plan. The Board and Exco can read it entry by entry and the
programme owner in full; section 3.4 gives the sequence.

Chapter 4 sets out which initiatives run, in what order, owned by whom, at what cost and with which
decision gates, for the programme owner, Steerco and the partners named in the RACI. Chapter 5 sets
out how progress is measured, through indicators, baselines, targets, data sources, the scorecard and the
2030 mid-term review, for Wesgro Research, the programme office and Steerco. Appendices A1 to A9
hold the traceability table, the demand model, the benchmark cases, readiness scores, the community fact
base, stakeholder inputs, charters, the toolkit and references, for analysts and anyone checking a figure.

Citations and figures
Every figure carries its source and page in a footnote, in the form Demand-Side Analysis, Jun 2026, p21.
The date is the cover date and the page is the PDF page index counted from the cover, because none of
the decks prints page numbers. The MIDAS Aviation Market Assessment and the three Skift memos have
no fixed pagination and are cited by section title.

Four cover dates differ from their file dates. The Demand-Side Analysis is cited as June 2026 though its
file is dated 1 October 2026 and the Inclusion Plan cites a 16 September version. The Infrastructure
Report cited is the July 2026 final, not the June draft. The SME and Community Inclusion Plan prints
September 2026 on a file dated 9 October, and the MIDAS Aviation Market Assessment prints March
2026 on a file dated 20 April 2026.

Where two inputs disagree the report states the value it uses, names the other with its page and gives the
reason. The five-market share, printed as both 63% and 61% on one slide, is one case. 203 Nothing is
averaged and nothing is reconciled silently.

Two arrivals figures recur and measure different things. The recovery baseline is 1.44m international
passenger arrivals at Cape Town International in FY24/25, ACSA's fiscal-year count.204 The growth model
runs from 1,498,829 arrivals in 2025205 to 2,175,778 in the base case and 2,516,282 in the accelerated
case in 2035.206

The strategy's 2.5m is the accelerated case rounded. The word doubling appears only where the report
quotes the original mandate.207

Highlighted text in square brackets marks an open item: a figure no input in hand carries, with the figure

cover carries a draft line; both come off at sign-off.

Three codes tie the chapters together. A Chapter 3 kicker names the entry's theme, 1.1 to 3.2, above the
focus area name as it reads on the tree [Summary of Findings, 14 Oct 2026, pp15 to 16: deck page
reference to be confirmed]; each entry closes with the initiative and indicator it links to. Initiatives in
Chapter 4 run I1 onwards in one sequence; indicators in Chapter 5 run T1-01 onwards for impact, T2-01
for outcome and T3-01 for output.

Appendix A1 holds the evidence behind the tree. For each focus area it records the workstream and
Chapter 2 sections behind it, rates the strength of the evidence and gives its initiative and indicator codes.
Any Chapter 3 claim can be followed back to a page and forward to an owner and a measure. Nine focus
areas rest on strong evidence and ten on adequate evidence. Two of the ten rest on indirect evidence:
focus area 5 has no fare or yield data, and focus area 15 is an inference from the pipeline tables. Focus

                        DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION




Chapter 2


Analysis and insights
Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                                              DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



Four parts build the case for the strategy in Chapter 3. Section 2.1 draws lessons from comparator
destinations; sections 2.2, 2.3 and 2.4 then examine demand, supply and the enabling environment, on
which Pillars 1, 2 and 3 rest.

Demand is not what holds the province back. Its shape and the system that serves it do. Five markets and
six months carry most overseas visits, and seats into Cape Town run out at the peak. Beyond the Metro,
product is hard to reach and book, and in townships and rural settlements the missing piece is a local
seller. The institutions that should hold the system together have thinned. Section 2.1 first shows how the
fastest growers dealt with their own binding constraint.

Stakeholder evidence runs through sections 2.2 to 2.4. The first of four engagement phases ran from
January to March 2026.208 In it, 16 organisations from Mossel Bay to the CTICC gave 223 rated insights:
115 High, 102 Medium and 6 Low.209 Each stakeholder statement in this chapter is the project team's
paraphrase of a point an organisation made, attributed to that organisation and never to a person. [Open
item: each organisation named here to approve its paraphrase before publication.] Each part ends with a
box, "What this means for the strategy", which sets out what the part's evidence means for the focus areas
it touches.


2.1 Global benchmarks and their lessons

The destinations that grew fastest ran institutions, marketing and air access as one system
aimed at a single binding constraint. For the Western Cape that constraint is seats into one
gateway; in its townships and rural settlements it is the lack of a local seller.

Benchmarks test the strategy against proven growth
The province aims to double international arrivals by 2035.210 Growth above trend is rare across whole
regions; where it happens, it is specific to a destination.211 Benchmarking asks which deliberate choices let
comparable destinations outgrow the world, and which of them the Western Cape can adapt. It looks for
how each destination removed what bound its growth, not for isolated good practice.212

The yardstick is the world's own growth. Growth of 3% to 5% a year is the norm under stable conditions;
above 5% marks structural acceleration and 10% or more a structural shift.213 UN Tourism puts global
growth at 11% in 2024, while recovery was still running, and about 4% in 2025.214 Growth counts as
structural only when it is "driven by deliberate changes to demand drivers, access conditions or product
systems, rather than cyclical recovery or short-term promotion".215 Each case was read for its demand
profile, two proven growth levers and how far they transfer to the Western Cape.216

Six comparators from a screen of 30
The search began with more than 25 destinations across Africa, Europe, the Americas and Oceania, and
30 were scored against 12 criteria in four groups.217 Market and access asks whether visitors arrive as the
Western Cape's do, by long-haul air through hubs from the same markets. Destination and product
structure asks whether a gateway city leads into several regions with a similar mix and price. Growth and

inclusion compares growth stage, seasonality, community participation and climate pressure; performance
compares volume, yield and infrastructure.218 Appendix A3 lists all twelve.

Each destination received one average score across the twelve criteria, and the 30 were ranked on it. 219
Four of the final six were among the six the screen judged most closely aligned: Chile, Melbourne and
Victoria, Valencia and Singapore.220 Below them, partial likeness ruled destinations out. Namibia lacks city,
wine and cultural depth. Reykjavik and Iceland sell one nature product with extreme seasonality, and the
Eastern Cape and Garden Route have no long-haul hub or global brand role.221

Score alone did not settle the set. The other two places went to New Zealand, ranked seventh at 3.67, and
Kenya, ranked eleventh at 3.50.222 Porto and Vancouver, which the screen had placed in its top six, did not
make the set, and no reason is recorded.223 The screen had judged New Zealand less aligned on source
markets and cruise, and Kenya weaker on wine and agri-tourism, with different pricing and governance.224
Kenya's inclusion was meant to make the set more balanced and regionally grounded.225 New Zealand's
comparability rests on access: like the Western Cape it is remote, long-haul and entered through one
gateway.226 Wesgro and the project team were to settle the list together.227

The six cover the constraints the province faces. Chile and New Zealand show how a gateway wins long-
haul seats and fills them; Singapore and Kenya show how hub traffic becomes visits. Melbourne shows
how events spread demand across the year, and Valencia how quality is governed.

Community cases and peer airports answer narrower questions
Five community cases ask what keeps tourism income in townships and rural settlements. Each matches
one of four types among the province's 107 long-listed communities.228 The types turn on two facts:
whether a community lies within 45 minutes of a tourism node, and whether it holds an attraction. Vilakazi
Street, Santa Marta in Rio de Janeiro and Kliptown speak to communities near a node. Costa Rica's rural
network and Comuna 13 in Medellín, remote by access rather than distance, speak to remote ones. 229

Cape Town's route record was set against eight peer airports: Calgary, Christchurch, Edinburgh, Keflavik,
Larnaca, Marrakech, Santiago and Valencia. Five are not their country's main airport, and most serve
seasonal or fast-growing inbound leisure markets.230 Santiago and Christchurch share Cape Town's
remote geography, and Santiago targets tripling its passenger volumes in twenty years.231

Cape Town wins more mainline routes than its peers
Between 2015 and 2025 Cape Town added 17 mainline international services, more than any of the eight
peer airports.232 Santiago added 14 services, Valencia 12 and Calgary 8.233 Yet winning routes is not the
same as holding seats through the winter, and the routes won now run full (section 2.3). Low-cost carriers
tell a different story: Edinburgh added 121 routes from 2015 to 2025, and low-cost carriers fly 81% of its
routes.234 At Cape Town, low-cost entry "would be a longer term opportunity".235 The Western Cape does
not need to learn how to win mainline routes. It needs what the leaders added: access funded as
infrastructure and marketing tied to each route.

Fast growth in 2024 was partly recovery
Only Chile and Singapore grew far faster than the world in 2024: Chile by 40.4% and Singapore by 21%,
against the world's 11%.236 Neither rate proves structural growth. Singapore was still at 86% of its 2019
arrivals, and most of Chile's arrivals came overland, 40% of them from Argentina. 237 Kenya, Valencia and
New Zealand grew only slightly faster than the world, and Melbourne's 4.3% is an Australia-wide forecast
rather than an actual.238 The lessons that follow come from what the leaders built over a decade, not from
one year's rate.

What each comparator faced and what it did

Chile built access first
Chile was chosen as a long-haul city gateway to coast, wine and wilderness, with Mediterranean seasons
like the Western Cape's.240 South America supplies 55% to 60% of its arrivals.241 Yet long-haul access was
its binding constraint, and Chile treated it as infrastructure.242 Santiago's airport traffic rose from about 16
million passengers in 2010 to more than 26 million in 2019.243 A private concession won in 2015 funded
about US$900 million of expansion, under one of the world's most liberal aviation systems built on open-
skies agreements.244 Direct routes now link Santiago with Europe and Australia, and a domestic network
carries visitors on to Patagonia and the wine valleys.245 The lesson is blunt: "Tourism demand follows air
access".246

New Zealand tied marketing to routes
New Zealand holds coast, wine, nature and culture within short travel distances, as the Western Cape
does.247 Air access economics bind its growth.248 It "shifted from awareness-driven marketing to route
accountable demand generation", tying budgets to specific long-haul corridors and designing campaigns
around airline breakeven and shoulder-season loads.249 The programme reports more than 16,000
incremental bookings and a return of about NZ$55 million in 2023/24. Government-backed early-stage
route incentives and data shared between tourism bodies and airlines support it.250 In the regions it curates
premium multi-day circuits and limits capacity to protect price.251

Singapore turned a hub into a destination
Singapore was chosen for joining air access, service quality, food culture and visitor segmentation in one
approach.252 Its question was how to turn transit through Changi into stays. Its answer is a stopover
programme, with the tourism board, Changi Airport Group and Singapore Airlines working "under a
coordinated strategy".253 It received 16.5 million international visitors in 2024 and record tourism receipts
of about S$29 billion.254 A hotel transformation map and fund lifted quality alongside, moving Singapore
"from lower-value stopover visits toward longer stays and higher-value tourism spending".255

Kenya made wildlife a governed system
Kenya is the African case, chosen for its long-haul reliance, regional dispersal and community-based
tourism.256 African markets supply 41% of its arrivals, while long-haul visitors spend more per head. 257
Kenya "institutionalised conservation as national economic infrastructure", joining wildlife protection,
tourism and community participation in one governed system.258 It positioned Nairobi as East Africa's
aviation, diplomatic and business hub, and its main airport now carries 68% of arrivals.259 Despite

Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                       DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



meaningful reforms, fragmented investment and regulatory hurdles remain ongoing challenges.260 Kenya
earned about US$1,460 per international arrival in 2024, against about US$890 for the Western Cape. 261

Melbourne and Victoria fill the year with events
Melbourne plays the part for Victoria that Cape Town plays for the province: main point of entry, brand
anchor and engine of demand.262 Summer still brings the largest spike in international demand, so Victoria
curates a portfolio of events that "generate repeat, predictable demand spikes across the year". 263 Behind
the calendar, hotel stock grew 21%, about 5,000 rooms.264 Melbourne "uses its event calendar to de-risk
and stimulate aviation demand".265 Its monthly share of arrivals ranges only from 7% in June and July to
12% in December.266 Its case rests on stability and yield rather than speed.

Valencia governed quality across the destination
Valencia, a coastal city of culture and food, matches the province on climate, product mix and price. 267 It
faced a familiar problem: fragmented governance and uneven quality across regions, operators and
seasons.268 It adopted a destination-wide quality system across its tourism businesses.269 Coordination sits
in "a permanent public-private structure led by Visit València", which joins municipal, provincial and
regional authorities in one decision-making system.270 It received 3.5 million international visitors in 2024,
13.9% more than in 2023 and 24.4% above 2019. Spend rose faster still, by 21%.271

The leaders treat air access as infrastructure
The Western Cape faces both halves of the access problem Chile and New Zealand attacked: too few
seats at the peak and thin demand off it. On the growth model, the base case passes a line of 1.8 million
inbound seats by 2030.272 That line is the model's assumption; in practice the runway binds at peak hours
(section 2.3). Off-peak, June carries only 6.4% of the year's international capacity. 273

The two cases point to three changes. Air access should be treated as "core economic infrastructure, not
a tourism add-on", with "a permanent, well-funded Public-Private Air Access Fund". 274 The province should
"actively lobby for air service liberalisation and simplified visa regimes".275 Marketing budgets should be
ring-fenced around priority long-haul corridors, with an aviation partnership framework and early-stage
route support.276

Transit and events can be turned into demand
Hubs can be converted into visits, as Singapore and Kenya show. Cape Town's seven largest
international markets are all hub airports.277 Government, aviation, events and industry should work to
shared KPIs, linking air access, major events and joint marketing.278 City, provincial and national actors
should align around one gateway strategy, with MICE treated as a system lever.279

Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                          DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



Events do the same work across the calendar, and the Western Cape has the green-season gap they
address (section 2.2). The province needs one event acquisition and calendar strategy across Wesgro,
the Convention Bureau, the City and the Province, with conference bidding aligned to sector strengths. 280

Quality and value are governed, not encouraged
The leaders manage quality as a deliberate outcome. Valencia and Singapore set standards through
standing bodies rather than leaving them to operators.281 The results reported for Singapore include longer
stays and higher spend per visitor; those for Valencia include more consistent service, a stronger
reputation and more repeat visits.282

The cases ask the Western Cape first for a province-wide quality and standards framework led by
Wesgro.283 Within it would sit a provincial quality charter phased by district, with certification "visible across
booking platforms", alongside standing sector councils for wine, MICE, adventure and culture.284 The
cases also ask the province to measure its regions by yield rather than arrivals and to build conservation
and community inclusion into the offer.285

In communities, lasting sellers matter more than capital
In communities the binding constraint is the seller. The five cases show that local sellers outlast the
programmes that launch them.286 In Santa Marta in Rio de Janeiro, about 20 resident guides ran the tours
by 2015 and outside jeep firms had left. The guides kept selling after the launch programme ended, while
assets and training without a route to market did not last.287 Access and safety were preconditions, not
product.

Scale brings its own limits. Medellín's Comuna 13 drew 1.98 million visitors in 2025, 52% of them
international, with about 300 professional guides. Mobility built for residents became the stage, but
carrying-capacity limits came late.288 On Vilakazi Street in Soweto, ten restaurants employ more than 800
people. Public space upgrades amplify existing assets, but benefits concentrate on street-front owners. 289

Neither capital nor donor money lasts without a seller that can stand alone. Costa Rica's community
network turned over more than USD 800,000 in 2013 and dissolved at the end of 2018; it did not survive
donor exit, though the record names no single cause.290 Kliptown in Soweto received a first allocation of
R436 million and was projected to draw almost 200,000 international visitors a year. By 2017 its museum
drew about 4,000 visitors a year, because capital built before an operator yields no operating income. 291

The cases converge on one order: "a seller with a stake, a safe and workable visit, product that keeps
spend in the community, then exposure".292 In the Western Cape's communities, the seller therefore
comes before the asset (section 2.4). In Doringbaai and Elands Bay, a local entity comes before more
product, with its own selling channel and a succession plan that does not rest on a donor. 293 Assets follow
proven demand.294

The system matters more than any lever
No single lever explains the leaders' growth. Sustained tourism growth "is not created by fragmented
interventions, but by a coordinated destination system in which governance, demand generation and
access reinforce one another".295 Chile's coordination works best "when anchored around a single
strategic constraint".296 The Western Cape's constraint has the same shape as Chile's long-haul access:
seats into one gateway airport (section 2.3).

Yet coordination is hard everywhere. Victoria reports tension between national and state bodies over roles
and branding.297 Valencia struggles to join its data across institutions.298 The Western Cape's three tiers of
government show the same strain (section 2.4). The strategy should anchor on one constraint, as Chile
did: peak seats into Cape Town first, one system built around them and, in the communities, a seller
before any asset.

What the comparison cannot show
The cases show what worked elsewhere; they cannot prove it will work here. Singapore's tourism board
answers to one ministry, in a system strongly directed by the state yet closely tied to the private sector. 299
The Western Cape works across three tiers, and air rights and visas sit with national government (section
2.4). New Zealand's governance differs too, and transfer means adapting governance, data and capability
to local realities.300

The evidence also has gaps. No score by criterion is printed, and among the six only New Zealand (3.67)
and Kenya (3.50) have a printed score.301 The margins behind the final choice cannot be checked. No
case of failure was studied. [Open item: add one comparator that underperformed, as the Inception
Report's plan asked (Inception Report, Nov 2025, p16).] Several comparator charts are unlabelled, so only
printed figures are quoted here, and Valencia's figures name no primary source.302 The recommended air
access fund carries no amount.303 None of the cases holds Western Cape data, so the province's side of
each comparison comes from sections 2.2 to 2.4. The direction of the lessons is robust; the precise rates
are indicative.

      What this means for the strategy
      Supplies precedent for FA07, Win direct routes and add seats on full routes, and FA15, Crowd
      in capital with targeted investment strategies. Chile's airport concession and air access fund; New
      Zealand's route-tied marketing.
      Supplies precedent for FA04, Convert India and China hub traffic, and FA06, Build a regional

      African base.

      events-led system and unified calendar.
      Supplies precedent for FA10, Support diversified route and product development. Chile's
      regional portfolio; New Zealand's premium circuits.
      Supplies precedent for FA11, Align visitor experience in-destination, and FA03, Make the
      whole province findable and bookable. Valencia's governed quality and certification visible on
      booking platforms.

      Resident sellers outlasted the programmes that launched them, and capital built before an operator,
      as in Kliptown, earned no operating income.




2.2 Demand-side factors

Interest in the Western Cape is broad and growing. Yet demand is concentrated in five
markets and six months. The growth markets fly in through Gulf and African hubs and book
on platforms where Western Cape product is hard to find.

A 2025 values-based survey reached more than 25,000 travellers in 34 outbound markets, 17 of them
Wesgro priority markets, with 800 to 1,600 in each.304 More than 20 discussions with market-facing
stakeholders added judgement to desk review of Wesgro reports, Statistics South Africa and Google
search data.305

Five markets carry the province and pay among the most
The UK, Germany, the USA, the Netherlands and France supplied 63% of overseas arrivals in 2025, and
the first three alone 52%.306 The pattern is not new: the top five held 61% of Cape Town overseas air
arrivals from January to August 2024.307
304
    Market Assessment Report, May 2026, p40.
305
    Demand-Side Analysis, Jun 2026, p7.
306
    Demand-Side Analysis, Jun 2026, p13; the slide prints 61% in its text, and 63% is the sum of its printed shares.
307
    Current State Diagnostic, Dec 2025, p29.


Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory                                                                                     48
Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                                           DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



The same markets pay among the most. Average spend per visitor in 2024 ran from R32,900 for the USA
and R26,500 for the UK down to R8,600 for Namibia and R8,500 for Zimbabwe.308 Only Nigerians and
Ghanaians spend as much, from a far smaller base (below). High-spend visitors come from Europe and
North America, "tying future value growth to slower-growing source markets".309 The Western Cape must
therefore hold yield in its mature markets while volume growth comes from elsewhere.

Six months carry three fifths of demand
Demand peaks in the southern summer and thins in winter. December, January and November took
12.7%, 11.5% and 11.3% of overseas visitors in 2025, while June fell to 6.1%.310 October to March hold
about 62% of the year.311 The 2024 Wesgro Visitor Tracking Survey gives a similar split of 61% and
39%.312

Growth therefore depends on "improving conversion in Apr-Sep rather than pushing incremental demand
into already busy peak months".313 Airlines cut seats in those same months, so filling the April to
September green season needs seats as well as conversion (section 2.3).

Removing barriers, not demand, sets the upside
The scenarios differ by intervention, not by demand: "the difference between scenarios is not demand
potential, but the extent to which structural barriers are removed".314 The growth model builds each
market's rate from four levers: outbound growth, the Western Cape's penetration, the airlift ceiling and
structural conditions such as currency and visas.315 It anchors on OAG aviation data for access and
traveller data for demand.316

From the 2025 base, the base case reaches 2,175,778 arrivals by 2035, implied growth of 3.8% a year.
The conservative case reaches 1,822,634, at 2.0%, and the accelerated case 2,516,282, at 5.3%. 317 The
base case's 3.8% sits within the 3% to 5% norm, and only the accelerated case clears the 5% mark of
structural acceleration (section 2.1).
