The step from 2,175,778 to 2,516,282 rests on confirmed direct routes to India and China, above-baseline
activation and more hub capacity into Cape Town.318 The dependency is hard: "without confirmed routes,
volume reverts to base".319 Those routes are not close. A direct Indian service is unlikely before 2030, and
China is a longer prize still.320 Until they come, the hub carriers carry the growth markets. Even the base
case passes the model's seat line at the 2030 year point (section 2.3).

Hub markets add most arrivals, and India and China grow fastest
In the base case the hub markets add the most arrivals and the acceleration markets grow fastest. The
hub markets are the UAE, Ethiopia, Qatar and Turkey. The acceleration markets are the Gulf, China, India
and Brazil ([Open item: Skift to confirm which Gulf markets the acceleration group holds, since the UAE
and Qatar sit in the hub group.]).321 Between 2025 and 2035 hub markets add 206,584 arrivals, mature
markets 178,780, acceleration markets 175,818, regional markets 110,298 and Japan and South Korea
5,469.322

India more than doubles from 76,547 to 165,260 arrivals and China with Hong Kong from 43,412 to
92,598, both at 8% a year.323 Spend follows the mix. The model fixes average spend for each group, from
R24,500 a visitor in mature markets to R8,500 in regional ones. On that basis base-case foreign spend
rises from a modelled R27.12bn in 2024 to R40.59bn in 2035.324 Volume and value come from different
markets.

The right segments can spread demand
Four values-based segments fit the Western Cape, together about 97 million addressable travellers. 325
Each has priority markets: Curious Explorers in the UK and the USA, and Creative Connectors in India.
Discerning Connoisseurs are paired with China and the UAE, and Reflective Adventurers with Germany. 326

Curious Explorers and Discerning Connoisseurs are "most aligned to the Western Cape's offering".327 The
segments also matter for spread, not only for volume. Curious Explorers offer the "strongest opportunity
for geographic dispersal beyond Cape Town"; Reflective Adventurers have "strong potential to support
seasonality mitigation" through year-round outdoor and wellness travel.328 These two are the segments to
target for the regions and the green season.

India and China already arrive through hubs
The growth markets are already in the air, on other carriers. About 71,000 Indian travellers and about
30,000 Chinese reach Cape Town each year through connecting hubs, together about 101,000
journeys.329 Addis Ababa and Dubai carry 81% of the Indian travellers, and half of them start in Mumbai. 330
Direct Indian service is "unlikely before 2030 due to aircraft constraints at Air India and IndiGo". 331 For
China, a China Southern service from Guangzhou is the likeliest first direct route.332

Australia (61,000 indirect travellers) and Saudi Arabia (137,000 passengers, with Riyadh Air's planned
hub) come next.333 The Western Cape should grow this hub traffic now. Singapore shows the method: its
tourism board, airport and airline work to one strategy (section 2.1). Here the partners are the hub carriers:
Emirates, Ethiopian, Qatar Airways and Turkish Airlines. Wesgro should build early relationships with the
Indian carriers and Indian government authorities.334 It should treat China as a long-term investment in
awareness and indirect volume before any non-stop service.335

Each market books on its own platforms
Conversion happens on platforms that differ by market. Chinese travellers discover on Douyin and
Xiaohongshu and book on Trip.com, Qunar and Fliggy. Indian travellers book through MakeMyTrip,
Booking.com and Expedia, and much Gulf travel still runs through private advisors and concierge
agencies.336

Each market also has its own gap. Cape Town has no direct flight to Mumbai or Delhi and thin Indian-
friendly adaptation, while Gulf travellers find no Arabic-language booking or local-currency pricing.337 Visas
add a further barrier, and they sit with national government (section 2.4).

The province's own channels also lag. The Department of Economic Development rates destination
platforms behind international practice in online presence and AI adoption. The Overberg says its
proximity to Cape Town is not packaged before travellers book flights.338 The money should therefore
follow the platforms: activate inside them and shift investment "toward trade-led conversion rather than
broad awareness campaigns".339

Africa is a base and a growth market
Closer to home, Africa already supplies a large and steady base. The province recorded 323,971 tourist
arrivals from 16 reported African markets in 2024, 62% of them from Zimbabwe and Namibia. 340 African
visitors spent R4.2bn in the province in 2025, and among them Nigerians and Ghanaians spent most per
visit, at R31,000 and R27,700.341 Namibia and Zimbabwe "add stability, repeat demand and counter-
seasonal volume".342

Nigeria, Kenya and Ghana are the priority growth markets, though the sources rank Ghana differently. 343
Nigeria's addressable market is modelled at 310,000 to 510,000 adult trips a year and Kenya's at 240,000
to 400,000.344 Africa is therefore both a regional foundation to keep and a source of high-spending visitors
to grow.

Business events can fill the trough if bids aim at it
Business events run on a different calendar from leisure travel. International association meetings in Cape
Town rose from 33 in 2022 to 58 in 2024. Of the 136 held over the three years, 109 fell between May and
November, but 74 fell in September to November alone.345 Most meetings therefore land at the edge of the
trough, not in it. Cape Town ranked 35th in the 2024 ICCA ranking and hosted 37 of South Africa's 64
UIA-recorded meetings in 2025.346 Hotel occupancy in Cape Town was 53% in June 2023, the gap such
events could fill.347

Yet MICE is not one business. Meetings, incentives, conventions and exhibitions have different buyers
and lead times. Only conferences are measured well, and meetings are "absent from consolidated
provincial statistics".348 Stellenbosch and George are credible secondary hubs that can take delegates'
extra nights while major events stay in Cape Town.349 The lever needs one events calendar, as Melbourne
has (section 2.1), bids aimed at May to August and measures for each of its four businesses.

Pillar 1 must fill the green season, win growth markets on the routes and platforms they already use, hold
yield in mature markets and build the African base. Seats, regional product and the enabling environment
(sections 2.3 and 2.4) decide whether that demand lands.

      What this means for the strategy
      FA01, Respond to growth segment traveller preferences. Four segments, each with named
      priority markets.
      FA02, Create green-season demand with MICE and events. Most international association
      meetings fall from May to November, but 74 of 136 fell in September to November. Bids must aim at
      the May to August trough. The seat side of the case sits in section 2.3.

      platforms that Western Cape product must reach.
      FA04, Convert India and China hub traffic. About 101,000 hub journeys a year and a direct Indian

      FA05, Increase yield in mature markets. The highest-spending visitors come from the slowest-
      growing markets.
      FA06, Build a regional foundation (Africa). A large regional base and two high-yield growth
      markets.




2.3 Supply-side factors

Supply sets the ceiling on growth. The gateway's busiest direct routes run full, the new
runway is years away and airlines are expected to raise fares before they add seats. Beyond
the Metro the product exists but is hard to reach, book or stay in.

Stakeholders rate supply constraints the most severe: 44 of the 68 insights on supply are rated High,
against 23 of 64 on demand.350 Two bodies of evidence test that view. Scheduled seats and estimated
bookings cover every Cape Town route, with bookings from the airlines' global distribution systems to
November 2025.351 On the ground, 50,332 tourism assets are mapped across six regions in three layers:
attractions, services and enabling infrastructure. The map draws on Wesgro's supply database, SANParks
and CapeNature data and open geographic sources. Its premise is that "destination performance is
constrained by the weakest layer".352 [Dataset limits, including the absence of geography below district
level, to be confirmed against the supply dataset notes.] Each region is scored from 1 to 5 on
attractiveness (40%), readiness and capacity (30%) and accessibility (30%).353

The model hits its seat line by 2030
On the growth model, seats run out before demand does. The model draws current air access as "a hard
ceiling at 1.8 million inbound seats". The base case passes that line by 2030, at 1,802,929 arrivals, up
from 1,673,855 in 2028.354 The line is the model's assumption, not a measured cap; what binds in practice
is the runway at peak hours (section 1.3). On the model's own judgement, even 2.1 million arrivals by
2035, below the base case (section 2.2), needs deliberate intervention to add capacity. 355 Growth to 2.5
million is therefore a supply programme before it is a marketing one.

    Stakeholder Insights Report, Mar 2026, p20 to p46, counted; insights filed under Transform Supply and Reshape Demand.
351
    MIDAS Aviation Market Assessment, Mar 2026, "The South African Market", "Seasonality"; bookings are weighted to the full market, so
they "will not always reconcile exactly with airport published data".

Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                                              DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



Cape Town's routes already run full
Cape Town has won more routes than its peers (section 2.1), and they now run full. Twenty-nine
international routes serve the airport in 2026, and Cape Town's share of South Africa's international
capacity rose from 10% in 2010 to 25%.356 In the year to November 2025, 11 of the 27 routes with load
data ran above 80%. Newark led at 88.6%, ahead of Atlanta at 84.3% and Frankfurt and London
Heathrow at 83.0%.357 Sixteen ran at 75% or more.358




Figure 2.4. Route load factors to November 2025
Source: MIDAS Aviation Market Assessment, Mar 2026, "Seasonality" (international route load-factor table: estimated bookings divided by
capacity, bookings for the twelve months to November 2025). Routes above 80% highlighted; the eleven routes below 75% are not shown.

Across all its routes, domestic included, Cape Town's 79% load factor is the joint highest of South Africa's
five largest airports, with Lanseria. South Africa averages about 70% and Africa 75%, and Cape Town's
international routes alone average 76%.359 Regional routes also run full, and all five markets are named for
more capacity. Luanda ran at 86.2% and 83.4% on its two airports, Maputo 83.3%, Gaborone 82.3%,
Mauritius 81.8% and Harare 81.3%. Domestically, Cape Town to Durban runs at 90.0% and warrants
more seats, while other domestic routes sit between 70% and 80%.360

Cape Town has reached "a 'what next' moment". Near-term seats must come from more frequencies and
larger aircraft on routes already full. Beyond that, the province needs a route strategy that "looks for long-
term gains rather than short-term wins".361

The network runs through hubs and skips Asia
Cape Town's seven largest international markets are all hub airports, with 2.7 million seats in 2026 against
2.0 million in 2019. Western Europe holds 38% of international capacity, the Middle East 23%, Southern

Africa 17%, Eastern Africa 14% and North America 6%.362 Most hub passengers connect: Emirates'
connecting share is "above 80% annually" and Qatar Airways' is nine in ten.363

New carriers lean towards Africa and the hubs. Seven of the twelve that launched Cape Town services
since 2014 fly to African points. By 2026 seats the largest are Airlink to regional points (441,602) and
Ethiopian to Addis Ababa (437,430). Turkish to Istanbul (240,200), United to Newark and Washington
(172,190) and Kenya Airways to Nairobi (127,138) follow.364

Yet no seat flies direct to Asia: the network shows "the absence of any direct capacity to any of the Asian
markets".365 Thirteen of the 27 routes in the load table serve African or Indian Ocean points, and none
serves Asia.366 A direct Asian route is therefore a long-term prize, and the near-term seats sit with the hub
carriers. The hub routes are not full. Dubai ran at 72.3%, Addis Ababa at 71.9% and Doha at 65.2% in the
year to November 2025.367 On those routes marketing can still fill seats, which it cannot do on the eleven
full ones.

Airlines cut winter seats and may raise fares
On full routes, airlines are expected to raise fares before they add seats. The forecast is that "carriers will
seek to increase revenues via higher selling fares rather than add new services", which "will frustrate that
desire for increased visitors".368 No fare data backs the forecast; it rests on judgement of airline behaviour.
In winter carriers withdraw seats. June carried 6.4% of Cape Town's 2025 international seats and
December 10.8%, with May, July, August and September at 6.9% each.369

Connecting traffic is "especially critical in the low season", which ties the winter schedule to the hub
carriers' own priorities.370 New seats must therefore be won route by route and season by season, as New
Zealand did with route-tied marketing (section 2.1).

One runway caps the gateway
The airport, not the airlines, sets the outer limit. Cape Town International's single runway has no parallel
taxiway and caps movements at about 30 an hour. Two of the airport's seven parts are already full. The
R6.39bn runway relocation lifts the cap to 45 movements an hour, a 50% rise. On the planning timeline it
completes around 2033, with terminal relief from about 2032.371 ACSA's latest schedule is earlier, with the
runway finished by December 2029, subject to contract award.372 This strategy plans on about 2033 and
treats the earlier date as upside.

The alternatives arrive sooner but carry less. George handled 911,000 passengers in 2025, above its
design limit. A R310m terminal expansion doubles its capacity to about 2 million passengers by 2028 or
2029. Cape Winelands Airport targets first passengers in 2028 on an R8bn first phase but has no firm
airline. Capacity and certainty pull apart: "the certain fix is the latest, and the earliest big-capacity option is
the least certain".373

Yet no stakeholder named Cape Town International's capacity as a constraint, and it appears only as a
transfer-time reference. No airline or airport sat among the 16 organisations, so the runway's absence
from their concerns is a gap in the record, not a finding.374 The outer limit is a national lever, held by ACSA
(section 2.4).

Cruise needs product, not berths
Cape Town's cruise berth has room, and port spending is not going to cruise. In 2024/25 the berth
handled 83 calls against a season capacity of 150 or more, with about 127,000 passengers and R1.79bn
of impact.375 Its limits are a 300m ship length, the South-Easter wind and no shore power.376

Mossel Bay handled 21 calls and about 13,000 passengers and opened a R22m passenger facility in
November 2025. Ships anchor and tender because it has no cruise quay, and only small and mid-sized
ships call. Saldanha Bay has had 3 trial calls and shares the ore terminal, with no cruise berth or
passenger facility. Its R4bn port upgrade serves iron ore.377 Mossel Bay's own count differs: calls rose
from four before COVID to 27 a season.378

Mossel Bay is ready for day stops, so the stance is to protect Cape Town, back Mossel Bay now and
watch Saldanha Bay. The province should grow cruise "through product and marketing, not capital
works".379

The flagships are full in season
The province's best-known sites run at capacity in summer. At the Table Mountain Aerial Cableway, peak
queues of up to three hours up and two down detract from the visitor experience. 380 Chapman's Peak
suffers disruptions, and the peak over December and January "overwhelms transport and accommodation

capacity".381 Boulders is "already near carrying capacity", and summit expansion on Table Mountain is
severely limited.382 Yet Cape Point and the township attractions are underused.383 Eleven of the 16
organisations see the offer as concentrated around Cape Town and a narrow set of icons.384 The peak
must therefore spread to product with room, under the capacity discipline New Zealand applies (section
2.1). The regions cannot yet take the overflow.

Readiness and access fall away from the Metro
Overall readiness halves between the Metro and the periphery. The Cape Metro rates 4.5 out of 5, the
Winelands and the Garden Route 3.4, the Overberg 2.7, the West Coast 2.5 and the Central Karoo 1.9. 385
Accessibility falls fastest. On attractions it scores 4.7 in the Metro, 3.3 in the Winelands and 3.0 on the
Garden Route. It drops to 2.0 in the Overberg, 1.7 on the West Coast and 1.0 in the Central Karoo, while
the Metro scores 5.0 on transport.386

Cape Town is both the gateway and the dispersal point. The Metro holds 20,281 of the 50,332 assets
(40.3%) and five of the province's six highest-volume attractions. It is the only region with an international
airport and rapid transit.387 It also generates 72.5% of provincial output, measured as GDPR.388

Beyond the Metro the visitor drives. Even the Garden Route, with its airport, has no shuttle between
towns. Public transport is "effectively absent" on the West Coast and in the Overberg, and the Winelands
have no transit between estates or valleys. Every accessibility measure in the Central Karoo "sits at the
floor", and even Metro rail is "functionally unusable for tourism".389 Stakeholders call weak public transport,
congestion and unreliable last-mile access "structural barriers to visitor movement within and beyond
Cape Town".390 Dispersal therefore "needs deliberate corridor investment, not organic spillover from Cape
Town".391

Strong regional assets lack beds and booking
The assets that could draw visitors outward sit where few can stay or book. The West Coast has the
province's highest natural-asset attractiveness and the Central Karoo its most unique niche assets, yet
both score lowest on readiness. Inland areas north of the N2 on the Garden Route are tourism-inactive.
Industrial belts in the Metro show "zero tourism conversion" despite nearby attractions. 392

Beds are one gap. In the Winelands mid-market accommodation "is effectively absent outside
Stellenbosch", and a "luxury-skewed product mix excludes mid-market and family segments".
Accommodation is thin beyond Langebaan and Paternoster on the West Coast and thin at international
grade along Route 62.393 No bed count yet sizes the gap.

Booking is the second gap, and it is widest in the regions. Six touring routes were rated Ready,
Developing or Gap on access, accommodation, safety and bookability (Emerging is the overall rating as
printed for the West Coast route).394 Route 62 has the lowest online bookability of the six, and the West
Coast route rates Gap on digital distribution and payments.395 CapeNature takes about 70% of its

The regions need beds and a booking route before marketing, with quality visible on booking platforms, as
in Valencia (section 2.1).

No committed money closes the regional gaps
Committed capital closes the air gaps, not the regional ones. The Metro's pipeline exceeds R30bn, led by
ACSA's R11.3bn airport overhaul, the R20bn V&A Granger Bay expansion and the R3.3bn MyCiTi Phase
2. Outside the Metro the money goes to roads, ports, airports and a few precincts: Cape Winelands
Airport, the George terminal, the R693m Malmesbury bypass, the R62 reconstruction and the R81.7m
Agulhas lighthouse precinct.398

None of it buys transit between towns, mid-market beds or a booking route. Section 2.4 shows that the
bodies that manage and sell the regions are short of money themselves.

Pillar 2 must therefore win seats into the gateway first, then build the corridors, beds and booking that
decide where growth lands.

      What this means for the strategy
      FA07, Win direct routes and add seats on full routes. Eleven routes run above 80% and fares are
      likely to rise before seats. The runway is the binding constraint until about 2033, and the base case
      passes the model's seat line by 2030.
      FA08, Grow cruise calls and ports. Cruise needs product, not berths.
      FA07 and FA15, Crowd in capital with targeted investment strategies. Airport capital is
      committed, but regional gaps have no committed capital.
      FA09, Accelerate tourism-enabling internal mobility. Accessibility falls from 4.7 to 1.0, and public
      transport is absent beyond the Metro. Stakeholders name last-mile access as a barrier, which
      supports it.
      FA10, Support diversified route and product development, and FA11, Align visitor experience
      in-destination. Strong assets sit in areas with little tourism use, and mid-market beds are missing.
      Stakeholders' account of a narrow product set supports FA10.
      FA12, Balance impact on nature-based attractions. Flagships are full at peak while alternatives
      are underused, as stakeholders also report.
      FA03, Make the whole province findable and bookable. Bookability is weakest in the regions, and
      stakeholders report product that cannot be booked online.
      FA02, FA04, FA05 and FA06, from the air side. Winter seats fall to 6.4% of the year in June. No
      seat flies direct to Asia today, and the hub routes still have seats, so conversion runs through the
      hubs. The mature long-haul routes are the fullest, and routes to five regional markets run above 80%
      and are named for more capacity.




2.4 The enabling environment

The enabling system is thinnest where the strategy needs it most. The decisive levers sit
nationally, local tourism bodies' funding has been disrupted and the province has no
approved way to count its tourism jobs. In the communities, organisation rather than
distance keeps visitor spend out.

The binding levers sit with national government
The levers that most limit growth are held nationally. In an insight rated High, the Department of Economic
Development reports that "restrictive bilateral air agreements are limiting airlift growth", leaving the
province less competitive than peers.399 Chinese routes are "frequently an extension of government-to-
government relations".400 The runway that caps Cape Town International sits with ACSA, a national body
(sections 1.2 and 2.3).

Visas and national marketing follow the same pattern. Chinese and Gulf travellers face slow, document-
heavy visa processes.401 Four organisations raised visas, among them the Cableway for India and China
and the CTICC for African events.402 The Department of Economic Development also finds that SA
Tourism's institutional weakness constrains international marketing.403 The Western Cape holds none of
these levers (section 1.2), so it must move them by escalation backed with evidence. Chile's case points
the same way: lobby for air service liberalisation and simpler visas (section 2.1).

    Stakeholder Insights Report, Mar 2026, p24; insights are the project team's statements, attributed to the organisation engaged.

Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                       DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



Funding has thinned and mandates blur
The bodies that manage and sell the destination are weaker than a decade ago. The Department of
Economic Development rates Western Cape and Cape Town tourism funding "materially below peer
destinations" for destination management and marketing. It finds that tourism institutional capacity "has
eroded over the past decade at both provincial and municipal level", and both findings are rated High. 404

National Treasury's MFMA Circular No. 131 of 31 March 2025 has since disrupted established municipal
funding for local tourism organisations (LTOs), a finding also rated High. Most of them "are not
operationally sustainable without municipal funding support".405 Mandates also blur at district level. The
West Coast District Municipality reports "a mandate disconnect" between the district, local municipalities
and LTOs that leaves tourism development without a clear owner.406

Fiscal pressure is turning attention to a tourism levy and blended finance.407 The committed capital in
section 2.3 builds airports, ports, roads, transit and precincts, but none of it funds the bodies that manage
and sell the destination. The strategy cannot rest on LTOs until their funding and mandates are settled.

Climate losses already fall on the flagships
Climate risk is now an operating cost at Robben Island and in CapeNature's reserves. Robben Island
Museum loses about R16 million a year to weather-cancelled trips, a High-rated insight.408 CapeNature
reports climate-related flooding, fire and heat closing sites.409 Sustainable destination management is
therefore risk management for the province's flagship product today, and should be funded as such, not as
a later add-on.

Collaboration platforms exist; data does not flow
Collaboration and data sharing are the barriers stakeholders raise most often. Fifteen of the 16
organisations raised industry and government collaboration, and fifteen raised policy and data sharing.410

Figure 2.7. Focus areas stakeholders raised

Data sharing also draws 30 insights and 16 High ratings, the most of any category.411 Stakeholders
describe the data architecture as "too fragmented, lagged and proxy-based to provide a reliable view of
visitor volumes, spend, spread or economic impact at provincial level".412

Coordination is hard everywhere, as the comparators show (section 2.1). Yet the platforms for
collaboration already exist here. FEDHASA points to JAMS, through which tourism associations
coordinate with Wesgro, the Western Cape Government and the City of Cape Town.413 What is missing is
an agreed way to share data across those bodies, not another forum.

The province has no approved jobs count
The province cannot yet measure tourism's jobs or its contribution to GDP. The national Department of
Tourism reports that "there is no approved official methodology for provincial tourism GDP and jobs
estimates", rated High.414 Stats SA cites 749 vacancies behind delays to the tourism satellite account and
infers provincial flows partly from ports-of-entry data.415

The communities show the same gap at small scale. No community profile counts tourism employment
inside its boundary. The figures are node-side instead, such as about 4,700 formal Town Centre jobs for
82,393 employed Mitchells Plain residents.416 Need rests on fifteen-year-old Census data.417 CapeNature,
the West Coast District and the Overberg raise skills gaps, but no labour analysis sits behind them. 418

For the Western Cape, a jobs baseline must come before a jobs target. The ambition of 300,000 tourism-
linked jobs (section 1.1) has no baseline yet. The yield-based regional metrics that New Zealand
recommends (section 2.1) also need a reliable view of spend and spread, which today's data cannot give.

Communities hold people, not spend
Foreign visitors spent R25.9bn in the province in FY2025, but little of it reaches its townships and rural
settlements.419 The 20 shortlisted communities come from a long-list of every Census sub-place cluster of
1,500 people or more with official unemployment of 25% or more. Named mission, fishing and farm
settlements are added.420 That gives 107 communities holding 2.81 million people in 2011, 48% of the
province.421 Five weighted criteria (need, tourism potential, proximity, readiness and access) produce the
shortlist of 20, five in the Cape Metro and three in each district.422

The 20 hold 18.4% of the province's residents and 15.4% of employed residents (Census 2011), yet
receive 0.02% to 0.04% of foreign direct spend.423 This comes to about R4m to R10m a year on the
documented floor, against R259m a year if they captured 1% of FY2025 foreign direct spend. 424

Inclusion here means activity inside the community. A tour, a meal, a bed or a market counts; a resident
commuting to a hotel job in town does not.425 Inclusion therefore turns on where visitor spend lands, not on
how many visitors come.

Organisation, not distance, keeps spend out
Distance is not the barrier. Of the 107 long-listed communities, 93 lie within 45 minutes of one of 35
tourism nodes or three corridors.426 Langa is 12 minutes from Cape Town International and Zwelihle seven
from Hermanus.427 Distance is the binding constraint in none of the 20.428 Yet proximity and access carried
35% of the shortlist score, so the result is partly built into the selection (Appendix A5, "Community shortlist
criteria").

Organisation is the binding constraint in 11 of the 20. Ten have no identified active tourism body, and nine
show no benefit visible to residents.429

Figure 2.8. Binding constraints in 20 communities
Source: SME and Community Inclusion Plan, Sep 2026, p30 (constraint tally across the 20 shortlisted communities).

Product often exists without a seller. Langa's walk sells on global platforms at ratings above 4.8, yet city
firms sell most of the 23 Langa walks on Viator.430 Zwelihle's four resident guides sell by phone with no
shared booking channel.431 The Mitchells Plain festival drew 27,800 visitors in November 2025 with no
local operator or LTO behind it.432 The communities cannot yet be found or booked on the channels
visitors use. What they lack is a local seller, not a shorter journey.

Safety is the second gate
After a seller, safety is the next gate a visit must pass. Nine of the 20 communities record a safety-
perception gap and five record extortion.433 Seven stakeholder organisations raised safety, a barrier that
runs across transport, township tourism and international selling.434 Tourism cannot fix this alone: "tourism
activity alone cannot resolve crime or extortion".435 Safety is therefore a launch requirement in its own
right, not an outcome of the pilot. No community pilot should open until it is met.

Each community type needs a different first move
The largest group of long-listed communities sits near a node and has something worth visiting, and each
type needs its own first move.436 Near a node with assets underused, 49 long-listed and 11 shortlisted
communities need sellers organised and a slot in the node's itineraries, procurement and events. Near a
node with nothing inside, 43 long-listed and 6 shortlisted communities need a seller first, then one anchor
asset under a covenant, then integration.

Remote communities with underused assets, 7 long-listed and 2 shortlisted, need access opened and the
asset placed on a route product the LTO already sells (Atlantis is classed remote by Wesgro's naming,

A seller with a stake comes first
The evidence points to a model built around a seller. It joins a functioning selling capability to demand the
node already has, behind launch gates for safety, access and a viable operating model.438 A pilot in two or
three of the six communities profiled first would test it. The six are Langa, Kayamandi, Zwelihle, De Rust,
Doringbaai and Laingsburg, one in each region.439 It would scale only once bookings, local spend, delivery
and safety meet thresholds agreed before launch.440

The first output is deliberately small: "one live booking route, published operating dates, a named
customer contact and a simple record of bookings and local revenue".441 Most first moves carry no
dedicated budget line.442 Chapter 4 sets out roles and funding. The order the cases support (section 2.1)
therefore becomes the order of delivery: a seller first, then a safe visit, then product and exposure.

Every gap in this part is a question of who benefits, who pays, who decides or who measures. Pillar 3
must settle those four questions, because Pillars 1 and 2 cannot deliver without the answers.

  What this means for the strategy
  FA13, Embed local destination bodies for community inclusion. The binding constraint is a seller
  with a stake, then a safe way in, then demand pulled from the adjacent node.
  FA14, Manage climate risk and sustainable tourism, and FA15, Crowd in capital with targeted
  investment strategies. Unfunded local tourism bodies and climate losses at flagship sites.
  FA10, Support diversified route and product development, and FA15. Assets follow sellers and
  route products, not the reverse.
  FA16, Align institutional stakeholders across all three tiers. Air rights, visas and SA Tourism sit
  nationally, Chinese routes follow government-to-government agreements and airport capacity sits
  with ACSA.
  FA19, Lead removal of infrastructure, visa and safety deficits, a governance task because
  visas and air rights sit nationally. Visas slow Chinese and Gulf travellers, and safety and extortion
  gate activation in up to nine communities.
  FA17, Strengthen industry and government collaboration, and FA18, Expedite tourism-
  enabling policy and data sharing. Each raised by 15 of 16 organisations, with existing platforms
  such as JAMS to build on.
  FA20, Develop workforce and service skills, waits on analysis still to be commissioned. Skills
  gaps raised by CapeNature, the West Coast District and the Overberg District have no labour
  analysis behind them. No count of tourism jobs exists, which also supports FA18.
  FA03, Make the whole province findable and bookable. Community product sells by phone or
  through city operators.

Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                                     DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION




3.1 Pillar 1: Reshape demand
Latent interest is not what holds Pillar 1 back; turning it into bookings is. The province is
known in its mature markets, under-sold in its growth markets and hard to book outside its
core.

Pillar 1 sets out to break through three ceilings on demand, in markets, seasons and spread, by reshaping
the brand toward new traveller segments. Five markets supplied 63% of overseas arrivals in 2025, and the
UK, Germany and the USA supplied 52% between them.443 June held 6.4% of the year's international
seats at Cape Town International in 2025, against 10.8% in December.444 Beyond the core routes, most
rural properties have no OTA presence and are invisible to the market.445

The gap between interest and arrival is wide. About 97 million high-value travellers in 34 markets have an
interest in South Africa, and about 1.4 million convert into arrivals.446 In the growth markets, "the constraint
is conversion and access, not only awareness".447 Each ceiling therefore needs a path from interest to
booking: green-season seats and events, bookable product and hub traffic that stops in Cape Town.

Two themes carry the pillar. The first, traveller, brand and proposition, decides whom the province sells to,
when and how. Its focus areas target four segments in their home markets, build green-season demand
from business events and make the whole province bookable (focus areas 1 to 3). The second theme,
source market growth and diversification, decides which markets grow the base. It converts Indian and
Chinese travellers already flying in through hubs, raises yield in the mature markets and builds the
regional African base (focus areas 4 to 6).

The two themes work as one. The segments set the target inside each market, and the market groups set
where volume and value come from. The green season and bookability decide whether the demand they
win lands in the months and places with room to take it. Initiatives I1 to I5 carry the six focus areas, and
section 4.3 sets out each one.

Pillar 1 carries most of the base case. Between 2025 and 2035 the base case adds 676,949 arrivals. 448
The four hub markets supply 206,584 of them, the mature markets 178,780, the acceleration markets
175,818, regional Africa 110,298 and the strategic group 5,469.449 None of it lands without seats, so the air
access focus area in Pillar 2 runs beside this pillar from the first phase (focus area 7). The Board will judge
the pillar on arrivals by market group, direct foreign spend and the green-season share, not on arrivals
alone (section 5.2).

3.1.1 Traveller, brand and proposition
The first theme is about whom the Western Cape sells to, when and how. It holds three focus areas: the
segments, the green season and the bookability of the province. Together they decide whether interest
becomes a trip: the right travellers, in months with room and on product they can book.

THEME 1.1 · TRAVELLER, BRAND AND PROPOSITION · FOCUS AREA 1


Respond to growth segment traveller preferences
Four traveller segments, each concentrated in named home markets, give Wesgro a sharper
target than country alone, and the province scores 3.7 to 4.6 out of 5 on its ability to win
them.

The opportunity
Country is too blunt a target for a long-haul destination. Within one market, travellers differ in what they
want from a trip, what they spend and where they book. Four values-based segments fit the Western
Cape. Together they hold about 97 million addressable travellers, and each is concentrated in named
home markets.450 Marketing to a segment inside a market puts each rand where the province is most likely
to win.

The prize to 2035 is value as much as volume. Curious Explorers and Creative Connectors together hold
over 40% of global outbound leisure demand, while Discerning Connoisseurs deliver the highest economic
impact per arrival.451 The segments also help with season and spread. Curious Explorers offer the
strongest opportunity for dispersal beyond Cape Town, and Reflective Adventurers support seasonality
mitigation through year-round outdoor and wellness travel.452

The measure holds the province back as much as the message. Performance is judged on arrivals. The
Department of Economic Development says current growth framing "overemphasises visitor volumes" and
that a value and spend lens may suit better.453

What the evidence shows

in the currency printed, which the documents do not name. The 1.4 million carries no year or source line.

The four segments rest on a May 2025 survey of more than 25,000 outbound travellers in 34 markets,
applied to 17 priority markets.454 Each segment has its own home markets, spend and booking channels,
so one campaign cannot reach all four. China holds the largest pool, about 21.49 million, but air access
and visas constrain its conversion.455

Yet two limits apply. The survey leaves out the domestic market and seven African markets, among them
Nigeria, Kenya and Ghana.456 Its findings are directional rather than definitive, and no Western Cape
validation exists yet.457 Stakeholders also name interests outside the four: medical tourism, township
culture, eco-adventure and international MICE.458 These are product interests that cut across the
segments, not a fifth segment.

The course of action
Wesgro should market by segment within market and judge results by value as well as volume. The UK
and Germany anchor the near term with a combined pool of about 12.5 million, and the USA is the largest
single pool outside China.459 The same research that tests the segments here should extend them to the
domestic and African markets, which the survey leaves out.

The order matters. Segment targets and validation come first, in 2027. The findings are directional, and
the budget should not move on them at scale until they hold for the Western Cape (section 4.3). Spend
moves to each segment's own channels from 2028. Spend per visitor joins Wesgro's targets from 2029,
once the data exists to track it.

THEME 1.1 · TRAVELLER, BRAND AND PROPOSITION · FOCUS AREA 2


Create green-season demand with MICE and events
The green season is a seat problem and an events problem at once. June holds 6.4% of the
year's international seats, and business events are the lever best placed to fill the trough,
though international meetings now peak from September to November.

The challenge
The green season is where the province has room to grow. Capacity sits underused in the green season
and across the regions.467 Cape Town hotel occupancy was 53% in June 2023.468 Growth to 2035
depends on "improving conversion in Apr-Sep rather than pushing incremental demand into already busy
peak months".469

Yet the trough is a seat problem as well as a demand problem. Airlines cut seats in the same months that
visitors fall away, and connecting traffic is "especially critical in the low season".470 Leisure marketing alone
cannot fill a month that carriers have already thinned (section 2.3). The province needs reasons to travel
that are fixed in the calendar, booked months ahead and sold with the seats.

Business events are the best of those reasons. They can be scheduled more flexibly than leisure trips, so
they can strengthen demand in shoulder and green-season periods.471 Two gaps remain: aim and
measurement. International meetings cluster just after the trough, and only conferences are counted well
(section 2.2).

What the evidence shows

Figure 3.1. Meetings, seats and visitors by month
Source: MIDAS Aviation Market Assessment, Mar 2026, Seasonality (share of 2025 international seat capacity at Cape Town International);

Seats and visitors both fall away from April to September. International meetings run thin from June to
August and peak from September to November, at the edge of the trough rather than in it. Events
therefore have one job: win new dates in May to September, where seats and visitors are thinnest.

Two limits qualify the numbers. The October to March share is about 62% for 2025, computed from a
month-of-travel table whose shares sum to 104.9% and normalised.472 The only printed split is 61% to
39% for 2024,473 and this report carries both. And only conferences are measured: meetings are absent
from provincial statistics, incentive volumes are unverified and exhibitions are unquantified.474

The course of action
Wesgro should build an April to September events spine and sell it with the seats. The spine rests on a
small set of flagship weeks, with green-season flight, stay and experience packages sold through airline
and trade partners.475 Bids follow the buyer. Conference bids aim at off-peak dates in sectors where the
province is strong. Incentive packages test the cooler season with Indian and Gulf planners, and winter
midweek packages target corporate meetings.476

The shared strategy and the count come first, in 2027. Wesgro agrees one event acquisition and calendar
strategy with the Convention Bureau, the City and the Province. It also sets up a way to measure
meetings, incentives and exhibitions. Without that count, no one can tell whether the calendar fills the
trough. It then publishes the calendar and aims bids at May to September (section 4.3).

Why it can work
Association conferences are won with long lead times, and they bring visitors who might not otherwise
consider the province.477 A congress confirmed for July gives carriers a load to plan against, and that is the
case Wesgro needs to hold winter seats (focus area 7). Events used this way sustain "year-round tourism
demand and air connectivity".478 They also reach beyond the city. The main event can stay in Cape Town
while delegates spend extra nights elsewhere, and Stellenbosch and George can host the next phase of
regional conferences.479

Melbourne's monthly arrivals stay between 7% and 12% of the year, a balance that events and education
travel help create. Yet summer still brings its largest international spike.480 Victoria builds the balance by
curating a portfolio of global, cultural, regional and business events that generate repeat, predictable
demand spikes across the year.481 The condition is the shared calendar and a proactive bidding pipeline
behind it.482 Without them, the gap stays where it is: June to August remains structurally weak for business
events.483

THEME 1.1 · TRAVELLER, BRAND AND PROPOSITION · FOCUS AREA 3


Make the whole province findable and bookable
Outside the Cape Peninsula, the Winelands and the Garden Route, most product cannot be
booked where travellers book, so interest the province has already earned has nowhere to
land.

The challenge
Interest that cannot be booked is lost. Travellers discover and book on platforms, and each growth market
uses its own (section 2.2). China books on Trip.com and Fliggy, India on MakeMyTrip and the Gulf on
Almosafer.484 Product that is missing from those platforms does not exist for the traveller, however good it
is. For the Western Cape this is the gap between Cape Town and the rest of the province.

The gap decides how far growth to 2035 spreads. Cape Town is the gateway, and the case for growth
beyond the Metro rests on visitors moving out and staying longer. The Overberg says it does not package
its offer early enough to be front of mind when travellers book flights.485 Each room or tour outside the core
that cannot be booked online depends on a traveller willing to pick up the phone.

The barriers are basic: online booking, card payment and rural broadband. Small operators, community
guides and even large public attractions still sell by phone. Two of the fixes sit outside Wesgro's reach:
rural broadband is a national programme, and national rules prevent bank-card payments on the George
bus service.486

What the evidence shows

Table 3.3. Evidence for focus area 3

      Bookability holds on the three core routes and falls away on the outer routes and in every profiled

ratings compiled from the community profiles, LTO confirmation outstanding); Stakeholder Insights Report, Mar 2026 (SIR). The 70% is
CapeNature's own reservation mix, not a provincial share.

Bookability is strongest on the three core routes where visitors already go. It is weakest on the outer
routes and in the communities the province wants to reach. Much of the gap is listing and payment:
guides, small operators and reserves exist, but many sell only by phone. Elsewhere the product itself is
missing: the Overberg and West Coast marine and wildflower assets have no guided tours and no
484
      Demand-Side Analysis, Jun 2026, p45, p47, p49.

      Stakeholder Insights Report, Mar 2026, p26.

      Stakeholder Insights Report, Mar 2026, p23.

Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                                                   DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



bookable product.487 Yet no count of bookable products exists.488 The indicator this focus area needs most,
the share of provincial product listed on OTAs by district, therefore has no baseline yet [bookable-product
count by district: Wesgro Research to build; see section 5.4].

The course of action
Wesgro should treat bookability as infrastructure: count it, close it district by district and then place it on
the platforms each market uses. Each pilot community's first deliverable is one live booking route,
published operating dates, a named customer contact and a simple record of bookings and local
revenue.489

The census comes first, in 2027, because the indicator has no baseline. Onboarding on the West Coast,
Route 62 and the Overberg follows from 2028. Packaged itineraries go onto market platforms from 2029,
once there is outer-district product to list (section 4.3).

Why it can work
Bookability turns interest the province has already earned into payment. It does not replace awareness:
the Overberg says potential visitors "do not sufficiently understand how close the district is to Cape
Town".490 Listing and packaging therefore go together. A listing on the platform where a traveller already
plans puts outer-district product in front of that traveller at the moment of decision. The census then
shows, district by district, whether the gap is closing.

New Zealand packages regional clusters into bookable long-haul itineraries and pairs regional operators
with specialist global travel designers.491 Its regions gain higher revenue per visitor and better seasonal
resilience.492 The lesson is to use Cape Town as a gateway that spreads value across the province
through bookable regional experiences.493 In communities, a booking route also needs a seller behind it. In
Santa Marta in Rio de Janeiro, about 20 resident guides ran the tours by 2015 and kept selling after the
launch programme ended.494 The condition is connectivity, payment and a seller with a stake.

3.1.2 Source market growth and diversification
The second theme is about which markets grow the base. It holds one focus area per market group: India
and China, which reach the province through hubs; the mature long-haul markets; and regional Africa.
Volume and value come from different groups, so each needs its own lever (section 2.2).

THEME 1.2 · SOURCE MARKET GROWTH AND DIVERSIFICATION · FOCUS AREA 4


Convert India and China hub traffic
About 101,000 Indian and Chinese travellers a year already reach Cape Town, mainly
through Gulf and African hubs, and with no direct Indian route likely before 2030, converting
that flow is the lever available now.

The opportunity
India and China grow fastest in the base case, at 8% a year to 2035, against 5% for the Middle East and
Brazil in their acceleration group.495 India and China start from a low base, at about 2.6% and 1.4% of
South Africa's overseas tourists in September 2025.496 The upside is large, and much of it is already in the
air.

About 71,000 Indian and about 30,000 Chinese travellers reach Cape Town each year through Gulf and
African hubs.497 They fly with carriers whose Cape Town business is built on onward traffic. Nine in ten of
Qatar Airways' Cape Town passengers connect through its hub, as do more than 80% of Emirates'. 498 Hub
routes through Dubai, Addis Ababa and Doha run at 65.2% to 72.3% load, so they have room for more. 499

The province is absent where these travellers decide. Both markets plan and book on their own platforms,
where Western Cape product is hard to find (section 2.2). Access and product add friction. Cape Town
has no direct flight to Mumbai or Delhi and thin Indian-friendly adaptation, while Chinese travellers face
complex visas and few Mandarin-language services.500

What the evidence shows

      India and China reach Cape Town only through hubs, and the direct route that would change that is at

The upside comes in two parts. The hub flow is available now, on routes with spare seats and carriers that
gain from every connecting passenger. The direct route is not: the accelerated case depends on
confirmed direct routes, and without them volume reverts to base.501 This focus area therefore works the
hub flow now and builds the route case for later.

The course of action
Wesgro should run India and China as conversion markets through the hubs and as relationship markets
for the direct route. The money shifts toward trade-led conversion rather than broad awareness. 502 India
needs early relationships with its carriers and authorities. China is a long-term investment in which
awareness and indirect volume come before any non-stop service.503

The hub work comes first because it needs no new seats. Wesgro agrees joint market support with the
three hub carriers in 2027, and activation on the Chinese and Indian platforms runs in market from 2028
(section 4.3). The direct route cases wait for runway capacity and go to carriers from 2032.

Why it can work
Conversion works here because the travellers are already moving. Each Indian or Chinese visitor routed
to Cape Town fills a seat the hub carrier already flies. The carrier gains from joint marketing without
adding capacity. Activation inside the platforms where these travellers plan and pay reaches them at the
point of decision, which broad awareness does not.

Singapore converts transit into visits through its Stopover Programme and Free Singapore Tour, run by
the tourism board, Changi Airport Group and Singapore Airlines under one strategy.504 New Zealand ties
marketing budgets to named long-haul corridors and designs campaigns to support route breakeven. It
recorded more than 16,000 incremental bookings and a return of about NZD 55m in 2023/24. 505 The
condition is visas and the hubs themselves. A Gulf hub disruption would cut connecting capacity, so the
work spreads across Dubai, Doha, Addis Ababa and Istanbul (section 4.8).

THEME 1.2 · SOURCE MARKET GROWTH AND DIVERSIFICATION · FOCUS AREA 5


Increase yield in mature markets
The mature markets already pay the most and fill the fullest routes, so growth there comes
from more value per visitor and more seats on existing routes, not from more marketing.

The opportunity
The mature markets are where the province earns its premium. High-spend visitors come from Europe
and North America, "tying future value growth to slower-growing source markets". 506 The mature group
grows steadily to 2035, but more slowly than the hub and acceleration markets (section 2.2). Growth here
comes from value per visitor more than from volume.

Value matters to the growth case because seats are scarce. Five mature long-haul routes already run
above 80% load: Newark, Atlanta, Frankfurt, Heathrow and Washington.507 The runway binds at the peak
until relief arrives in about 2033 (section 2.3). Each extra rand per visitor on a full route is growth that
needs no extra seat. Longer stays and travel beyond the Metro add value in the same way.

Two things stand in the way: carrier pricing and the province's own measures. On full routes, carriers are
expected to raise fares rather than add new services, which "will frustrate that desire for increased
visitors".508 The province must therefore earn more per visitor without losing share. Yet its targets count
arrivals, and spend per visitor by market is printed for a single year.

What the evidence shows

The constraint is well documented; the lever is not. The five routes are full and their visitors pay most. Yet
none of the work behind this report holds fare, revenue-per-seat or yield data. None models a yield uplift
either. The yield lever rests on how carriers are expected to behave on full routes. This focus area
therefore sets yield as an indicator to establish, not a target to hit, until spend per visitor by market is
tracked every year [yield baseline: SAT spend per visitor by market as an annual series; only 2024 is
printed].

The course of action
Wesgro should protect share and raise value per visitor in the mature markets, and work the full routes for
frequency and gauge before it chases new ones. The task is to protect share, increase yield and drive
regional dispersal while new growth markets develop.509 There is tactical room for added frequency on
some routes and larger aircraft from some airlines.510

The full routes come first. From 2027 Wesgro makes the frequency and gauge case to their carriers,
because seats held now protect share until the runway relief arrives (section 4.3). Luxury-advisor
distribution follows in 2028. Spend per visitor and length of stay join Wesgro's own targets from 2029,
once the data-sharing protocol supplies them (focus area 18).

Why it can work
Yield work suits full routes. When seats are scarce, a visitor who stays longer, travels further and spends
more adds value without adding a seat. Selling to the highest-spending segment through luxury advisors
lifts the average spend of the travellers the seats already carry. Full routes also give Wesgro a frequency
case to put, though not a promise of seats, because carriers are expected to raise fares first. 511

New Zealand runs yield-based performance, putting revenue per visitor and length of stay ahead of
arrivals growth and selling premium regional itineraries through specialist travel designers.512 Its visitor
spend rose 9.2% in the year to March 2025.513 The condition is a measure. Without spend per visitor by
market as an annual series, yield can be neither managed nor judged, so the data-sharing protocol comes
first (focus area 18).

THEME 1.2 · SOURCE MARKET GROWTH AND DIVERSIFICATION · FOCUS AREA 6


Build a regional foundation (Africa)
Regional Africa is the province's steadiest volume and its least developed premium market:
Zimbabwe and Namibia supply 62% of African arrivals, while West African visitors spend the
most per visit.

The opportunity
Africa is the province's nearest market and its steadiest. Namibia and Zimbabwe "add stability, repeat
demand and counter-seasonal volume".514 Regional trips are shorter in lead time and easier to convert,
which helps the green season.515 Africa is therefore both a base to keep and a market to grow (section
2.2).

The opportunity to 2035 is premium growth on top of that base. Nigerian and Ghanaian visitors spend as
much per visit as visitors from the mature markets, from a far smaller base (section 2.2). Cape Town is not
a cold start in Nigeria or Kenya, where awareness of South Africa exists and needs converting rather than
building from scratch.516

Access is the constraint. Routes to Luanda, Maputo, Gaborone, Harare and Mauritius run above 80%
load, so added demand there meets full aircraft.517 Visas add friction: the CTICC reports that regional
African event demand is constrained by visa requirements.518

Why it can work
The two-market approach matches the lever to the demand. On the five full regional routes, volume is
capped by seats, so the capacity case is where it grows. Premium demand in Nigeria and Kenya already
knows South Africa, which ranks second only to Dubai in Kenyan outbound consideration.522 Activation
converts that awareness rather than having to create it. Conferences with leisure extensions serve both,
because business travel can help smooth demand across the calendar and event attendance can become
longer stays.523

Kenya splits its demand in a similar way. Regional African markets drive its volume and business travel,
while long-haul markets bring leisure demand and higher spend per visitor.524 It draws 41% of its arrivals
from Africa, mainly from Uganda and Tanzania.525 Nairobi serves as East Africa's aviation, diplomatic and
business hub through Kenya Airways' regional network.526 The transferable changes are to treat MICE as
a system lever and to align city, provincial and national actors around one gateway strategy. 527 The
condition is seats and visas. Activation in Nigeria and Kenya converts only travellers who can get a visa
and a seat.

3.2 Pillar 2: Transform supply

Supply sets the ceiling on the growth case. The airport caps arrivals before its relief arrives,
and outside the Metro the product exists but is hard to reach, book or stay in.

Pillar 2 removes the mobility, infrastructure and product constraints that limit how many visitors the
province can carry and where they can go. Demand work in Pillar 1 fills seats only if the seats exist. It
pays outside the Metro only if visitors can reach, book and stay in the product there (section 2.3).

The first constraint is the gateway. The growth model draws current air access as a line at 1.8 million
inbound seats, and the base case crosses it at the 2030 year point.528 The runway binds at peak hours
before that.529 The international terminal upgrade at Cape Town International is due about 2032 and the
relocated runway about 2033.530

Transport is a connectivity and dispersal challenge, not a province-wide capacity crisis. Cape Town
International is the key gateway risk.531 Beyond the gateway, the constraint is reach and readiness.
Accessibility to attractions and services falls from 4.7 out of 5 in the Cape Metro to 1.0 in the Central
Karoo.532 Only one touring route of six is rated Ready.533

The pillar holds two themes. The first is how visitors reach the province and move through it (focus areas
7 to 9). The second is what they find on arrival (focus areas 10 to 12).

Only the air entry needs more transport capacity. Cruise has room at the berth: it handled 83 calls in
2024/25 against a season capacity of 150 or more.534 The flagship nature sites face a limit of a different
kind, which calls for managing flows rather than adding volume (focus area 12). The other three entries
concern reach, product and experience. The entries also have an order. Seats into the gateway come first;
corridors, beds and booking then decide where growth lands (section 2.3). Visas, air service agreements
and the runway are national levers, which the province leads by escalation in Pillar 3 (section 3.3.2).

By 2035 the pillar must do two things. It must hold and add seats on the full routes until runway relief
arrives. It must also lift the share of arrivals and spend outside the Metro, by giving visitors product they
can reach, book and stay in. Initiatives I6 to I11 deliver the six entries (section 4.3).

3.2.1 Accessibility, connectivity and infrastructure
The first supply theme covers how visitors reach the province and move through it. Air access and cruise
bring visitors in, and internal mobility carries them beyond Cape Town.

THEME 2.1 · ACCESSIBILITY, CONNECTIVITY AND INFRASTRUCTURE · FOCUS AREA 7


Win direct routes and add seats on full routes
Eleven of 27 international routes run above 80% load and carriers raise fares before seats,
so the province must win frequency and larger aircraft now, ahead of runway relief in about
2033.

The challenge
Cape Town International is the province's only international airport, so the growth case runs through one
gateway.535 On the growth model, seats run out before demand does, at the 2030 year point. 536 Growth to
2035 is therefore a supply programme before it is a marketing one (section 2.3).

Two limits bind before that line. The first is commercial. Cape Town's best routes already run full, and on
the mature long-haul routes carriers are expected to raise fares rather than add services.537 The second is
physical. A single runway caps movements at about 30 an hour, and the relocated runway that lifts the
cap is due about 2033.538 ACSA's latest schedule is earlier, with the runway finished by December 2029,
subject to contract award.539 This strategy plans on about 2033 and treats the earlier date as upside.

Yet the province starts from strength. It added 17 mainline international routes between 2015 and 2025,
more than any of eight peer airports.540 The task is to hold and enlarge those routes until the runway
arrives, and to have the next ones ready when it does.

What the evidence shows

      The network is full on its best routes, the airport is full at its runway at peak, and planned relief lands after

The evidence describes a timing problem more than a demand problem. The fares forecast covers the
mature long-haul routes only, and no fare data yet tests it.541 The five regional markets that also run above
80% are named for more capacity.542 The hub routes still have room, with Dubai at 72.3%, Addis Ababa at
71.9% and Doha at 65.2%.543 On those routes marketing can still fill seats.

Relief also comes in the wrong order: "the certain fix is the latest, and the earliest big-capacity option is
the least certain".544 George doubles its capacity but flies domestic routes only, and Cape Winelands has
no firm named carrier.545 No airline took part in the first phase of stakeholder engagement, so carriers'
views on frequency and gauge are untested.546

The course of action
The approach splits the air agenda by horizon. Until relief arrives in about 2032, the job is to fill and
enlarge what already flies. The near-term moves are added frequency from Emirates, Qatar Airways and
Ethiopian Airlines, with airline incentives coordinated through Cape Town Air Access. Route cases for
India and China are the long-term move.547 Wesgro also presses for more seats to Luanda, Maputo,
Gaborone, Harare and Mauritius, the five regional markets above 80%.

The fund is designed in 2027 and 2028, so it is running when the regional frequency cases are made from
2029. The India and China cases go to carriers from 2032, as runway capacity arrives (section 4.3). ACSA
delivers the runway and terminals, and Wesgro tracks them quarterly as the first risk. Air service
agreements, the cap on US carriers and immigration processing sit with national government, so the
province escalates them (section 3.3.2).

Why it can work
The programme works on the airline's decision, not the visitor's. A carrier on a mature long-haul route
seeks revenue through fares, yet there is tactical room for added frequency and larger aircraft. 548 Joint
marketing and a ring-fenced fund lower the carrier's risk on extra capacity. The province then buys seats,
not higher fares. New Zealand tied marketing budgets to long-haul corridors and designed campaigns

THEME 2.1 · ACCESSIBILITY, CONNECTIVITY AND INFRASTRUCTURE · FOCUS AREA 8


Grow cruise calls and ports
Cruise already brings about R1.79bn a year with Cape Town's berth at about 40% of its
season capacity, so growth comes from ships, excursions and regional day stops, not from
port capital.

The opportunity
Cruise has room to grow at the berth Cape Town already has. The equipped berth handled 83 calls in
2024/25 against a season capacity of 150 or more, and the season brought about R1.79bn of economic
impact.552 Growth here needs product and marketing, not capital works.553

Cruise also carries spend beyond the city. Cape Town anchors the itinerary, and Mossel Bay and
Saldanha Bay can extend it east and west.554 Stakeholders name home porting and expedition vessels as
the segments that bring longer stays and higher local spend.555

What limits growth is ships and product, not berths (section 2.3). The equipped berth takes ships of up to
300m, the South-Easter delays berthing and there is no shore power.556 Port capital goes to freight:
Saldanha's R4bn iron-ore expansion and Cape Town's container expansion add no cruise capacity.557

What the evidence shows

      Cape Town's berth has room, ship size and wind are the constraints, and port capital is going to freight.

about 23 calls next season.559 Yet ships anchor and tender there, and only small and mid-sized ships

One number is unresolved. The plan to grow to 250 calls has no source in the evidence. Cape Town's
season capacity is about 150 calls. Reaching 250 would need a second berth, a longer season or the
regional ports counted in. No document describes any of these [plan to 250 cruise calls: source, horizon
and definition to confirm with Cruise Cape Town]. The strategy carries the 2024/25 baseline and holds the
target open until the source is in hand.

The course of action
The approach works with the berth Cape Town has rather than waiting for a new one. Cruise Cape Town
leads, and the sequence follows readiness. Line targeting and the Mossel Bay excursion packages are
designed in 2027, together with a sourced definition of the 250-call plan. Marketing to expedition and
home-porting operators follows in 2028, alongside community product on cruise-operator channels
(section 4.3). Saldanha Bay waits until demand is proven, because its port capital serves iron ore.

The port itself stays with its owners. Transnet National Ports Authority and the V&A Waterfront hold berth
scheduling, wind delays, shore power and the 300m limit. The national ask is that Transnet puts cruise
into port planning, since current Western Cape port capital carries none.

Why it can work
The actions match the constraint. The berth has headroom; the gaps are ship-size flexibility, weather
reliability and tourism product.562 Each line won with ships that fit adds calls without capital works.
Packaged excursions give passengers more to buy ashore, and regional day stops spread that spend
along the coast.563

The best precedent is local. On its own count, Mossel Bay's calls rose from four before COVID to 27 a
season, supported by closer coordination and better excursion packaging.564 The town credits its
partnership with Wesgro.565 None of the six benchmark destinations was chosen for cruise.566 The nearest
analogue is Singapore's stopover programme, in which the tourism board, the airport and the airline share
one strategy to turn transit passengers into visitors.567 The condition is that today's ship deployments hold.
Recent growth leans on Red Sea reroutes, and their reversal is the main risk.568

THEME 2.1 · ACCESSIBILITY, CONNECTIVITY AND INFRASTRUCTURE · FOCUS AREA 9


Accelerate tourism-enabling internal mobility
Accessibility falls from 4.7 out of 5 in the Cape Metro to 1.0 in the Central Karoo, and
beyond the Metro and the Garden Route the province is effectively car-only.

The challenge
Growth to 2035 has to spread beyond Cape Town, yet large parts of the province outside the Metro and
the Garden Route are effectively car-only.569 The N2 is the only continuous inter-regional tourism corridor,
a single point of failure.570 Limited public transport "restricts access for non-driving visitors and reduces the
addressable tourism market".571 Section 2.3 shows readiness and access falling with each step away from
the Metro.

The problem is reach, not capacity.572 Even in the Metro no rail line reaches the airport, and safety
perceptions and limited visitor wayfinding hold back Metrorail's tourism potential.573 Stakeholders call weak
public transport, congestion and unreliable last-mile access structural barriers to visitor movement. 574

No single body owns the visitor's journey. The Department of Mobility notes that mobility responsibilities
are split across three tiers of government, and argues the case for a dedicated transport authority. 575

What the evidence shows

Table 3.9. Evidence for focus area 9

      Access falls away with distance from Cape Town, and the rail and road assets that exist are not run for

The shuttle gap has a commercial fix. A shared shuttle needs demand aggregation and booking
integration to work, as !Khwa ttu says of the West Coast.579 Part of the answer is therefore within reach of
a marketing and packaging body, while the rail and road assets stay with their owners.

The course of action
Wesgro does not build transport. It makes existing transport usable for visitors and gathers the demand
that makes shuttles pay. The Department of Mobility leads the work, with Wesgro's Tourism unit. The
Southern Line product, shuttle aggregation and transfers sold inside itineraries are piloted in 2027 and
2028. Shuttle products run from 2029, as the City and PRASA complete the Central Line recovery and
MyCiTi Phase 2. The Cape Town to George rapid rail decision and the last mile to Cape Point come later
in the period (section 4.3). National government is asked to lift the rule that bars bank-card payments on
the George bus and to fund passenger rail beyond the Metro.

Why it can work
The mechanism is aggregation. A shuttle pays only at volume, and pooled bookings across operators,
sold inside a multi-day itinerary, turn scattered requests into a load that can run. The Southern Line needs
no new track to become a product, only branding, passes and marketing. Each step widens the market to
visitors without a car, the market that limited public transport now narrows. 580

None of the six benchmark destinations carries an internal mobility lever with figures.581 The Cape
Winelands already shows the mechanism at home. Its heritage tram, built to connect wine estates, solves
the drink-and-drive barrier and is a one-of-a-kind product.582 Yet the tram model is not replicated
elsewhere.583 Medellín shows the principle at city scale. Mobility built for residents in Comuna 13 became
part of the visit. The area drew 1.98 million visitors in 2025, 52% of them international, though governance

3.2.2 Product and destination capacity
The second supply theme covers what visitors find once they arrive: product beyond the icons, the quality
of the stay and the limits of the flagship sites. The three focus areas work as a set. New product gives
visitors a reason to travel outward, quality standards make that product safe to sell and flow management
at the flagships sends the overflow to it.

THEME 2.2 · PRODUCT AND DESTINATION CAPACITY · FOCUS AREA 10


Support diversified route and product development
The province does not lack attractions; it lacks finished experiences around them, with
assets outside the Metro left without guides, interpretation or bookable product and one
touring route of six rated Ready.

The challenge
The growth case needs visitors to travel beyond the Cape Peninsula, stay longer and spend in more
places. The province has the assets to support that. Of 50,332 mapped tourism assets, the Cape Metro
holds 40.3% and the five other districts the rest.585 The West Coast has the highest natural-asset
attractiveness in the province and the Central Karoo its most unique niche assets.586

Yet both score lowest on readiness, and the product around the assets is unfinished (section 2.3). Eleven
of 16 organisations engaged see the offer as concentrated around Cape Town and a narrow set of
icons.587 The Department of Economic Development adds that destination development has focused more
on managing barriers than on building product outside the Cape Peninsula.588

Concentration costs the province twice. The flagships run full in season while Cape Point and the
township attractions are underused.589 Further out, strong product sits where visitors cannot easily reach
or afford it.590

What the evidence shows

Table 3.10. Evidence for focus area 10

      Assets are spread across the province, but product, packaging and investment stop at the core routes.

The evidence locates the constraint. It is less about creating new attractions and more about turning

The course of action
Wesgro works outward from strength. The Garden Route, the Cape Winelands and the Overberg already
have strong underlying product and need connectivity, packaging and service depth.594 Route 62 and the
West Coast are packaged as slow-travel products. In 2027 and 2028 the Tourism unit designs bookable
itineraries for all six touring routes. It starts where bookability is weakest: the Whale Coast east of
Hermanus, Route 62 and the West Coast. Delivery at scale starts on the three corridors, then moves to
Route 62 and the West Coast (section 4.3).

to play.595 The capital sits with others. Accommodation investors and municipalities add beds and service
depth in secondary towns later in the period (focus area 15). National government and SANParks carry
rural broadband and precinct projects.

Why it can work
The actions supply what the assets lack: a guide, a bed and a booking route. Packaging turns scattered
assets into a trip a visitor can buy, and a multi-day itinerary gives a reason to stay outside the Metro. The

Overberg shows the mechanism at home: its whale-watching anchor drives operator confidence despite

THEME 2.2 · PRODUCT AND DESTINATION CAPACITY · FOCUS AREA 11


Align visitor experience in-destination
The province offers luxury estates and basic self-catering with no mid-scale branded tier
between them, and service, interpretation and language readiness thin out with distance
from Cape Town.

The challenge
The growth case needs visitors the current offer serves poorly. Province-wide, no midscale branded
product sits between luxury estates and basic self-catering.599 In the Winelands, a luxury-skewed product
mix excludes mid-market and family segments.600 The Garden Route is short of quality-graded hotels that
anchor midweek, conference or business travel.601

The experience is weakest at the arrival gate and at the periphery. The international airport is the most
negative node in Cape Town's online mentions.602 Further out, readiness falls with distance from the Metro
(section 2.3). Accommodation readiness drops from 4.7 in the Metro to 2.0 in the Central Karoo. 603

The growth markets add their own needs. Chinese visitors find few Mandarin-language services. Indian
visitors see little sign of vegetarian options or Indian-language guides. Gulf visitors find no Arabic-
language booking or local-currency options.604 These are the markets Pillar 1 courts, so the gaps sit on the
growth path (section 3.1).

Two measures are missing. No source counts mid-scale rooms or beds [mid-scale room count by district:

The course of action
The quality charter comes first, because it sets the standard the other actions aim at. DEDAT leads it with
Wesgro's Tourism unit (section 4.3). It is a province-wide framework that aligns all tourism stakeholders
under consistent benchmarks.607 It is phased by district, with certification visible on booking platforms and
support for SMMEs to comply.608 Design runs through 2027 and 2028, and the district phases start in 2029
(section 4.3).

The investment case for mid-scale hotels follows the charter from 2029, so that investors build to a known
standard (focus area 15). The arrival gate sits outside the province's reach: Home Affairs and ACSA hold
immigration processing and peak-hour throughput at Cape Town International, so the province presses
them.

Why it can work
A visible standard solves a problem of trust. A visitor booking a guesthouse on Route 62 cannot judge its

confidence.

Quality governance was Valencia's primary lever. Its Qualitur programme and SICTED quality system
raised service consistency, reputation and repeat visitation.609 The city received 3.5 million international
visitors in 2024, 13.9% more than in 2023 and 24.4% above 2019.610 Singapore's tourism board used a
hotel industry transformation map, a hotel rejuvenation fund and the Hotels Act. The aim was to move
from short stopovers towards longer stays and higher spend.611 The condition is that small businesses can

THEME 2.2 · PRODUCT AND DESTINATION CAPACITY · FOCUS AREA 12


Balance impact on nature-based attractions
Boulders is near carrying capacity and Cableway queues reach three hours, so growth at
the flagship nature sites must come from managing flows and steering visitors to under-
marketed reserves.

The challenge
The flagship nature sites carry the province's brand. Table Mountain, Robben Island and Kirstenbosch
form the only globally branded landmark cluster, and the Metro holds five of the province's six highest-
volume attractions.612 In season they run full (section 2.3). Peak queues at the Cableway of up to three
hours detract from the visitor experience.613

More volume at these sites is the wrong goal. The Department of Economic Development wants further
growth directed to under-visited parts of the province rather than constrained flagships.614 Yet the flagships
congest at the peak while the overflow reserves remain under-marketed.615

Nature tourism also funds conservation. CapeNature cannot rely on public funding alone and treats
tourism as a revenue business to support its conservation mandate.616 Flooding, fire and heat are already
closing CapeNature sites, so the reserves that should take the overflow are themselves under strain. 617
