Why it can work
Flow management protects the visit and the revenue together. Digital flow tools and tiered pricing spread
arrivals across the day and the season. A site then serves its visitors without a three-hour queue and
earns more at the peak. Marketing the alternatives at the point of booking sends the overflow to the under-
marketed reserves, and their income pays for conservation there.

New Zealand uses land-use controls and premium curation on the principle that capacity discipline
protects long-term pricing power.620 Kenya treats conservation as national economic infrastructure, with
tourism-funded conservation financing and community conservancies that sustain demand and
environmental integrity.621 It earned about US$1,460 per international arrival in 2024, against about
US$890 in the Western Cape.622 The City already runs the model at small scale. Since August 2025
Witzands permits have been capped by day, and 80% are pre-booked online.623 The condition is the
operators. They must share their counts, and their mandates must allow pricing: CapeNature's statutory
affordability mandate has kept prices conservative and is moving it towards tiered pricing. 624

3.3 Pillar 3: Build enabling capability

Pillar 3 decides whether the other two can be delivered and shared: who benefits, who pays,
who decides and who measures.

Pillar 3 sets out to build an inclusive, well-governed tourism system that supports sustainable growth,
returns and development. The other two pillars depend on it. Demand work fills seats and supply work
opens the regions, but neither lasts unless someone owns the decisions, pays for the assets and counts
the results. Section 2.4 found that system thin at each point the strategy relies on.

The pillar answers four questions: who benefits, who pays, who decides and who measures. On the first,
20 township and rural communities hold 18.4% of the province's residents but receive a traced 0.02% to
0.04% of foreign direct spend.625 On the second, the capital pipeline funds airports, ports, roads and rail,
and none of it reaches tourism product, bookability or community assets.626 On the third, visas and air
service agreements sit with national government, and mobility mandates are split across three tiers. 627 On
the fourth, no approved methodology exists for provincial tourism GDP and jobs.628

Two themes and eight focus areas carry the pillar. The first theme, investment, inclusion and
sustainability, settles who benefits from growth, how it is paid for and how it lasts. It gives community
product a local seller, manages climate risk through funded local bodies and builds an investment pipeline
that follows demand (focus areas 13 to 15). The second theme, governance and leadership, settles who
decides and who measures. It aligns the three tiers of government, turns industry forums into one joint
plan and agrees how data is shared (focus areas 16 to 18). The same theme holds two further focus
areas. Focus area 19 takes the national deficits in infrastructure, visas and safety to the tier that holds
them. Focus area 20 commissions the labour analysis a skills plan will rest on.

The eight entries work as a sequence, not a menu. Governance comes first. Steerco, the data-sharing
protocol and the escalation agenda start in the first quarter of 2027, because every other initiative reports
through them (section 4.2). The community pilots start in the same phase, with booking routes shared with
focus area 3 and safety plans drawn from focus area 19. Climate risk, local tourism funding, the
investment pipeline and the labour analysis spend 2027 and 2028 on design. Each first needs a baseline
or a case that does not yet exist. Capital comes last. It follows demand that the pilots and the regional
product have shown, not the reverse (section 2.1).

The evidence behind the pillar is mostly qualitative. It rests on statements attributed to the 16
organisations with recorded insights, a one-page table of what each tier holds, the community profiles and
the district gap tables.629 The pillar therefore carries more open items than the other two. Several entries
start by building the baseline they will be judged against.

By 2035 the pillar must leave the province able to deliver its growth and share it. Visitor spend should
reach the communities, measured against a reference point of 1% of foreign direct spend, R259m a year
at FY2025 levels (section 5.3). Local tourism bodies should hold stable funding, and tourism projects
should reach financial close. The national tier should answer the province's asks, and the province should
count its own tourism jobs and spend. Initiatives I12 to I18 carry the eight focus areas, with focus areas 16
and 17 sharing one forum in I15 (section 4.1).

3.3.1 Investment, inclusion and sustainability
The first enabling theme covers who benefits from growth, how it is paid for and how it lasts. It holds three
focus areas: community inclusion, climate risk and investment. The inclusion model is set out at concept
level, and Chapter 4 assigns its roles and funding.

THEME 3.1 · INVESTMENT, INCLUSION AND SUSTAINABILITY · FOCUS AREA 13


Embed local destination bodies for community inclusion
Twenty communities hold 18.4% of the province's residents but receive only a small
documented share of foreign direct spend, and in eleven the binding constraint is
organisation, not distance or product.

The opportunity
Community inclusion decides whether growth to 2035 is shared. Inclusion here means visitor activity
inside the community: a tour, a meal, a bed or a market. A resident commuting to a hotel job in town
counts for little or nothing.630 On that measure the province starts near zero. Foreign visitors spent
R25.9bn in FY2025, and the 20 shortlisted communities received a documented floor of R4m to R10m of
it.631

The prize is large. At 1% of FY2025 foreign direct spend, the 20 communities would receive R259m a
year; at 1% of the 2035 spend ambition, R547m.632 Both are reference points, not adopted targets (section
5.3). The visitors are already close, and organisation rather than distance keeps their spend out (section
2.4).

Inclusion also widens what the province sells. SATVITO finds that more township and village product
could widen market reach and lift visitor spend, yet negative safety perceptions of the townships hold
demand back.633 The opportunity opens only to a community that can sell and a visit that is safe.

What the evidence shows

The evidence places the main gap in selling, not proximity. Organisation binds in 11 of the 20

and bookability.635 The spend floor is uncertain, because the method behind it is not yet shown [Open
item: the Inclusion Plan to set out how the R4m to R10m floor was traced.]

The cases set the order of action: a seller with a stake, then a safe and workable visit, then product that
keeps spend in the community and only then exposure.636 Kliptown in Soweto shows the cost of the
reverse order. Walter Sisulu Square cost R160 million to R180 million, out of the R436 million first
allocated to Kliptown, before an operator was in place (section 2.1).637 By 2017 it drew about 4,000
museum visitors a year, against a business case of almost 200,000 international visitors.638 Safety is the
second gate. In Rio, security collapsed in 2017 and public support was withdrawn early.639 Nine
communities record a safety-perception gap and five record extortion, so every pilot needs a site-specific
safety plan before launch.640

The course of action
Wesgro should connect community product to the demand it already moves, and leave the building of
selling bodies to municipalities, LTOs and the communities themselves. The model has three parts: a
selling capability that works, a link to demand that already exists and gates that must be passed before
activation and investment.641

In 2027 Wesgro and its partners choose two or three pilots on readiness and agree who funds a seller
with a stake in each. Safety plans follow in the same year (section 4.3). In 2028 each pilot opens one live
booking route and runs a fixed-date test, and Wesgro places the product in its itineraries and calendars.
Investment follows demand, and pilots scale from 2029 only once bookings, local spend, delivery and
safety meet thresholds agreed before launch.642

Why it can work
The model supplies the missing piece and little else. Demand already passes within minutes of most
communities. A local seller with a booking route turns that passing demand into bookings, and the gates

THEME 3.1 · INVESTMENT, INCLUSION AND SUSTAINABILITY · FOCUS AREA 14


Manage climate risk and sustainable tourism
Climate is already costing the province sites, routes and revenue, while the local tourism
bodies that would manage the response depend on municipal funding that MFMA Circular
No. 131 has disrupted.

The challenge
Growth to 2035 rests on sites and routes that weather can close, and the losses already fall on the
flagship sites (section 2.4). Each district carries its own climate risk, from water security on the Garden
Route to extreme summer heat in the Central Karoo.650 Water scarcity acts as a hard ceiling on visitor load
in high-growth nodes such as the Garden Route.651 A risk spread across every district needs an owner in
each.

Yet the bodies that should manage the response locally are the weakest link. The Department of
Economic Development rates Western Cape and Cape Town funding for destination management and
marketing materially below peer destinations. It also finds that most local tourism organisations (LTOs)
cannot sustain operations without municipal funding, which MFMA Circular No. 131 has disrupted. 652 A
body unsure of next year's budget cannot plan for a flood or a fire season.

their municipal funding before and after Circular 131: DEDAT to supply]. The LTO funding finding rests on

The response exists in pieces. Mossel Bay's membership inspections check water-wise and green
practices, the only working sustainability standard on record.654 CapeNature is reassessing asset formats
and siting to cut losses from flooding, fire and extreme weather.655 What is missing is a provincial record of
losses and a stable owner for local destination management.

The course of action
Wesgro should treat climate as a destination-management risk it records and plans around, and press for
stable funding of the local bodies that do the work. In 2027 and 2028 DEDAT and Wesgro's Research unit
build a climate and weather risk register for sites and routes, starting with the dated losses (section 4.3).
They also design an LTO funding model that answers Circular 131. Wesgro routes itineraries around
Meiringspoort while it is closed. From 2029 funding agreements with a development mandate are signed,
and routing around closures draws on the register. The quality charter carries sustainability checks on the
Mossel Bay model (focus area 11). From 2032 climate risk and LTO funding sit in municipal IDPs, with
reporting.

Three districts have positions to build on. The Cape Winelands can become a climate-smart wine region,
the Overberg a nature-positive test bed and the Garden Route a case for resilience investment. 656 The
register comes first because routing, funding and capital choices all depend on knowing which sites close
and how often.

Why it can work
The register turns scattered losses into decisions. Once losses are dated and costed by site, Wesgro can
route itineraries and marketing around a closure. Operators can plan capital around the risk. CapeNature
finds off-grid solar and self-sufficiency increasingly necessary to keep remote sites running.657 Stable
funding works on the other half. An LTO with a multi-year agreement and a development mandate can
plan for a fire season, not only the next campaign.

The evidence diagnoses this risk more than it prescribes for it. No comparator carries a climate-adaptation
or local funding lever, so the precedents are partial. Kenya finances conservation from tourism, a self-
financing system that supports conservation and sustains tourism demand.658 Valencia runs its SICTED

THEME 3.1 · INVESTMENT, INCLUSION AND SUSTAINABILITY · FOCUS AREA 15


Crowd in capital with targeted investment strategies
The province has a capital pipeline for airports, ports, roads and rail, but none of it reaches
tourism product, bookability, accommodation depth or community assets, which is where the
growth case needs investment.

The challenge
The growth case needs capital where the current pipeline does not reach. Visitors who travel beyond the
Metro need beds, a booking route and finished product. The communities need assets a local seller can
run. Yet committed capital closes the air gaps, not the regional ones (section 2.3). The province does not
lack capital works. It lacks capital aimed at tourism.

Tourism funding is also tight. The Department of Economic Development rates Western Cape and Cape
Town funding for destination management and marketing materially below peer destinations. Fiscal
pressure is prompting consideration of a tourism levy.660 The Department of Mobility sees a need for
climate, development, blended-finance and private-sector models to fund tourism-enabling mobility.661
Much of the product gap will therefore fall to private capital, which moves only where a case is ready.

The accommodation gap shows what is at stake. Province-wide, no mid-scale branded product sits
between luxury estates and basic self-catering, and the Garden Route is short of quality-graded hotels for
midweek, conference and business travel.662 Mossel Bay reports that limited upper-upscale hotel capacity,
particularly a recognised brand able to host conferences, holds back its higher-value and business
demand.663

What the evidence shows

The evidence shows a gap in direction as much as in volume. The district tables list a large capital
pipeline, and their gap columns show that none of it reaches tourism product. That finding is an inference
from the tables rather than a printed statement, and no source gives a Western Cape tourism funding-gap
figure [Western Cape tourism funding against the peer set: DEDAT and Wesgro budget data needed].
Kliptown is the warning case: capital built before an operator earned no operating income (section 2.1).

The instrument the evidence calls for has not yet been built. An investment opportunity framework would
score infrastructure and service gaps against return, job creation and inclusion.664 Its roadmap would set
out quick wins, medium-term plays and long-term moves.665 The district gap-closure tables are its ready
input.

The course of action
Wesgro should build the tourism investment pipeline the gap tables imply, and commit public capital only
where demand has been shown. In 2027 and 2028 Wesgro's investment promotion team scores the gaps
for return, jobs and inclusion and prepares the first investment cases (section 4.3). These cover mid-scale
and conference-capable hotels in secondary nodes and accommodation at De Hoop and on Route 62.
From 2029 it takes the cases to investors, and DEDAT and the City of Cape Town test a tourism levy and
blended-finance vehicles. The pipeline is refreshed each year.

One rule runs across the portfolio: invest after demand, and attach a maintenance and marketing
covenant to any public asset before it is let or upgraded.666 The order matters. A levy or a blended-finance
vehicle needs projects to fund, so both are tested only once the first cases exist.

                      and invest in community assets only after demand is proven
 National             Hold ACSA, PRASA and Transnet to their published capital dates, and       ACSA; PRASA; Transnet

Why it can work
A scored pipeline lowers the cost of finding a deal. Investors see ranked cases with demand evidence
attached, rather than a list of district needs. Investing after demand reverses the Kliptown sequence, and
covenants keep a public asset maintained and marketed after it opens. Wesgro has done this before. It
brokered IDC funding for the Doringbaai abalone farm in 2015; the village trust holds 37% of it, and staff
grew from eight in 2013 to about 50.667

The comparators pair public direction with private capital. Chile funded a ~US$900 million expansion of its
gateway airport through a private concession awarded by its Ministry of Public Works.668 Melbourne paired
its event strategy with 21% growth in hotel rooms, 5,000 new rooms and the entry of global hotel

3.3.2 Governance and leadership
The second enabling theme covers who decides, who measures and how the plan is run. It holds five
focus areas. Three build the means of joint decision: three-tier alignment, collaboration with industry and
shared policy and data (focus areas 16 to 18). Two apply them to specific gaps. Focus area 19 escalates
the national deficits in infrastructure, visas and safety. Focus area 20 builds workforce skills on a labour
analysis still to be commissioned.

THEME 3.2 · GOVERNANCE AND LEADERSHIP · FOCUS AREA 16


Align institutional stakeholders across all three tiers
Air service agreements and visas sit with national government, mandates are split across
three tiers and the national marketing body is under strain, so the province needs one
shared agenda with clear decision rights.

The challenge
The levers the growth case needs are spread across three tiers of government, and none of them answers
to one plan. The national tier holds visa rules, air service agreements and route rights. The province holds
jobs, safety programmes and infrastructure planning. Local government holds basic services and precinct
management.671 The levers that bind growth most sit nationally (section 2.4). The province must therefore
move decisions it does not make.

The bodies that should carry this work have thinned. The Department of Economic Development finds that
tourism institutional capacity has eroded over a decade at provincial and municipal level.672 Even working
partnerships lapse. FEDHASA reports that the Discover Garden Route MOU has expired, and that Knysna
municipality's non-participation limited its reach.673 Without one provincial voice, each ask reaches the
national tier from several directions or not at all.

What the evidence shows

      The levers the growth case needs are spread across three tiers, and the bodies that hold them are thin or
      out of step.

The evidence agrees on the problem and is thin on the fix. What is needed is "a shared growth agenda
with clear decision rights and joint delivery plans".674 Yet no source maps decision rights by tier beyond a
single summary table. The national view on air rights, visas and marketing is also inferred from provincial
and local voices, not heard directly.

On these levers the national tier is barely heard. The Department of Tourism and Stats SA spoke only to
data, and SA Tourism is listed as engaged with no view recorded.675 The first task is therefore to hear the
national tier directly and write down who decides what.

The course of action
Wesgro should convene the shared agenda and hold the evidence the national tier needs, rather than try
to own levers it cannot. In the first quarter of 2027 the Wesgro CEO's office and DEDAT constitute a
Tourism Growth Steerco (section 4.2). Wesgro's CEO and DEDAT's Head of Department co-chair it, with
Cape Town Tourism as a standing member (section 4.7). Steerco agrees a decision-rights map by tier and
tables the first national escalation agenda (focus area 19). It gives the province one voice and one route to
national decisions.

Route work runs alongside, inside existing agreements, through standing relationships with Indian carriers
and authorities and with China's state-owned carriers (section 4.4).676

Why it can work
Alignment works by settling ownership before it is contested. A decision-rights map shows each tier what it
owns, so provincial and local bodies stop waiting on decisions that are theirs to make. One forum then
speaks for the province, so national bodies receive one position rather than several. Where the
comparators name the body that negotiates air service agreements, it is national: Chile's civil aviation
board and Singapore's aviation authority.677 The province's lever is evidence and alignment, not authority.

Coordination already pays at local scale. Mossel Bay grew cruise calls from four before COVID to 27 a

associations coordinate with Wesgro, the Western Cape Government and the City of Cape Town through

embeds municipal, provincial and regional authorities in one decision-making system.680 The change
asked of the Western Cape is to embed tourism governance in permanent public-private structures. 681 Yet
coordination strains even among the leaders: in Victoria, roles and branding remain a source of tension
between national and state bodies.682 The condition is national engagement. The plan works only if the
national tier takes part in Steerco's agenda and answers its asks, which section 5.2 tracks.

THEME 3.2 · GOVERNANCE AND LEADERSHIP · FOCUS AREA 17


Strengthen industry and government collaboration
Fifteen of the sixteen organisations engaged raised collaboration, and the channels already
exist in JAMS, the bid teams and the tour-operator forums, so the task is to turn existing
forums into one joint plan.

The opportunity
Collaboration is one of the two concerns stakeholders share most widely, and the channels for it already
work. Fifteen of the 16 organisations engaged raised it, as many as raised data sharing (section 2.4).
FEDHASA reports that Western Cape tourism associations already coordinate through JAMS with
Wesgro, the Western Cape Government and the City of Cape Town.683 C-BRTA cites biannual tour-
operator forums, and the Department of Tourism cites statistics committees that already convene public
and private stakeholders.684 The province does not need another forum. It needs the forums it has to plan
together.

Joint work already pays where the task is concrete. Mossel Bay's cruise calls rose from four before Covid
to 27 a season, supported by closer coordination and better excursion packaging.685 The town credits its
public-private tourism model with faster decisions.686 The CTICC finds the same on bids: destination
stakeholders collaborate effectively on bid development and international event attraction.687

Growth to 2035 needs that discipline applied to development, and here the system falls short. The West
Coast District Municipality finds provincial and regional forums dominated by destination marketing rather
than development.688 Operators are represented only through LTOs and business chambers, so private
participants are sometimes unaware of tourism plans and projects.689 The Department of Economic
Development reports that misalignment between public tourism institutions and key industry bodies is
weakening collaboration.690

What the evidence shows

Table 3.17. Evidence for focus area 17

      Collaboration is wanted almost everywhere and already works in places, but the forums are marketing-led
      and the trade's voice is missing.

The record describes a system that collaborates to sell and stalls when it must plan. Joint work succeeds
on a bid, a cruise call or a route, where the task is bounded and the payoff shared. It weakens on
development, where no forum owns the agenda and operators sit one step removed. The airlines and
distributors that fill the seats are also absent. No airline, online travel agency or tour operator took part in
the first phase of engagement.691

The count behind that breadth is rough. It records organisations with at least one collaboration insight, not
the weight of each.692

The course of action
Wesgro uses the forums that exist and gives them a development agenda and one joint plan. In 2027 it
adopts JAMS as the single channel to the associations, because FEDHASA expects consultation through
it to cascade more efficiently than approaching each body separately.693 It adds the Tour Guides
Association, so frontline operators have a voice.694 Sector councils for wine, business events, adventure
and culture are designed inside Steerco's structure (section 4.7) and run from 2029 (section 4.3).

Partners carry the regional and bidding work. District municipalities, DEDAT and the LTOs give regional
forums a development agenda and tailor provincial support to district needs. The CTICC and the
universities supply the academic and sector champions on whom international bids depend.695 Nationally,
the province asks SA Tourism to include provincial assets in its campaigns for emerging source markets,
which Robben Island Museum also seeks.696 From 2029 the joint industry plan is agreed and reviewed
each year.

Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                                   DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



Why it can work
A joint plan gives each forum a decision to make, not just information to exchange. Sector councils give
wine, business events, adventure and culture a standing seat, so the market, not officials, sets
development priorities. Shared targets already pay here. Cape Town secured 36 conference bids in
2024/25, expected to bring more than 27,000 delegates and R745 million in economic impact through
2028.697 Cape Town also added 17 mainline international routes from 2015 to 2025, more than any of
eight secondary airports in the comparison. That record is credited to Wesgro's commercial and
relationship-driven route development.698

Valencia shows the model at destination scale. Sector-led executive committees, with the regional
business confederation on the governing board, keep tourism development market-driven rather than
bureaucratically led.699 Valencia received 3.5 million international visitors in 2024, 24.4% above 2019.700
That performance is credited to governance that is decentralised, sector-led and embedded across the
destination.701 Singapore's tourism board, airport group and national airline work under one coordinated
strategy with shared KPIs.702 The condition is commitment from industry. The plan holds only if the
associations and the trade sign up, so the number of partners signed up is its measure of success (section
5.2).

Link forward
Initiative I15, Tourism Growth Steerco and three-tier alignment (section 4.3). Indicator T2-22 industry
partners signed up to the joint plan at outcome level (section 5.2).

THEME 3.2 · GOVERNANCE AND LEADERSHIP · FOCUS AREA 18


Expedite tourism-enabling policy and data sharing
No approved method exists for provincial tourism GDP and jobs, operators cannot see their
own customers and four arrivals measures run unreconciled, so a data-sharing protocol is
the precondition for every target.

The challenge
The province cannot yet measure what the strategy sets out to grow. The Department of Tourism reports
no approved method for provincial tourism GDP and jobs estimates. Official tourism GDP and employment
data have also lagged since Covid-era method changes, it adds.703 Data sharing draws more stakeholder
insights than any other theme, as section 2.4 shows. Bodies from Stats SA and SA Tourism to ACSA, the
airlines and the card schemes produce tourism data. Yet they collect different metrics at different
frequencies with different definitions.704

Every target to 2035 depends on fixing this. The plan tracks spend, season and spread beside arrivals. No
source yet prints visitor spend by district (section 5.3). Even arrivals run on four measures that are not
reconciled to one another: ACSA passenger arrivals, SA Tourism tourist arrivals, OAG air arrivals and the
growth model's international arrivals.705 Without a shared data layer, no intervention can be targeted or
measured.706

Policy and the operators' own data are as thin. The Department of Economic Development reports a
provincial tourism policy gap, with limited legislation and out-of-date master-planning frameworks. 707 At the
sites, third-party ticketing channels do not share visitor-level data with the Table Mountain Aerial
Cableway.708 Robben Island Museum cannot link its marketing spend to visitor acquisition.709

The evidence locates the gap in sharing, not in supply. Provincial macro-research is strong; what is

in progress, with owner and start date: Wesgro Research and DEDAT to confirm].

Part of the gap is national and beyond the province's reach. Stats SA cites 749 vacancies as one factor
that appears to slow the tourism satellite account.711 The province can agree terms with data holders now.
A recognised provincial jobs and GDP method needs the Department of Tourism.

The course of action
Wesgro's Research unit agrees data-sharing terms with each data holder and runs one indicator register
and dashboard, as section 5.5 sets out. In 2027 it builds the register and agrees terms with ACSA, SA
Tourism, Stats SA, Cape Town Tourism, the site operators and the booking platforms. In 2028 the
dashboard goes live with automated arrivals, seat and cruise feeds and quarterly digital tracking (section
4.3). The dashboard sets campaign results against airline capacity and hotel trends, and annual or twice-
yearly brand research sits beside it.712 Wesgro also starts to measure meetings, incentives and
exhibitions. Meetings are absent from consolidated provincial statistics, and the scale of the other two is
unverified.713 From 2029 Nigeria and Ghana join the growth model (section 4.3).

Partners and the national tier hold the rest. Attraction operators and CapeNature share visitor-level data
from their ticketing and reservation systems. DEDAT and the municipalities close the provincial policy gap
and embed tourism in IDPs and spatial plans, with reporting, as the West Coast District Municipality
asks.714 Nationally, the province asks the Department of Tourism to approve a provincial tourism GDP and
jobs methodology and Stats SA to resource the tourism satellite account.

THEME 3.2 · GOVERNANCE AND LEADERSHIP · FOCUS AREA 19


Lead removal of infrastructure, visa and safety deficits
Visas, air rights, the runway and policing sit with national government, so the province leads
by escalation backed with evidence, and delivers the safety programmes and basic services
that are its own.

The challenge
Visas, air rights, airport capacity and policing shape how many visitors reach the province and how safe
they feel. The province holds few of these levers. National government holds visa rules, border
compliance and air service agreements. The province holds safety programmes and local government
holds basic services.718 ACSA, a national body, holds the runway (section 2.4). Escalation is therefore the
province's main tool for the national share.

The deficits bear hardest on the markets the growth case needs. Chinese and Middle Eastern travellers
face slow, document-heavy visa processes.719 The Table Mountain Aerial Cableway cites visas as a
constraint on growth from India and recovery from China, and the CTICC on regional African events. 720

Inside the province, safety and basic services gate both visits and community growth. Nine of the 20
shortlisted communities record a safety-perception gap and five record extortion. A formal safety
assessment remains a separate requirement before any community product launches.721 Congestion costs
the Metro an estimated R2.8 billion a year, and water scarcity acts as a hard ceiling on visitor load in high-
growth nodes.722 Here the province and the municipalities hold part of the answer.

What the evidence shows

Stakeholders raise the deficits widely. Ten of the 16 organisations raised them, and safety perception
alone came from seven.723 Yet even this evidence, broad as it is, is statement rather than measure. No
visa processing time is in hand for any priority market (section 5.2).

National reform can move arrivals. Ghanaian arrivals to South Africa reached 36,656 in 2024, up from
prior years, a rise attributed to the visa waiver724 [Ghana visa waiver start date, given as 1 November 2023
and as 2024: Wesgro Research to confirm]. E-visa expansion and the Trusted Tour Operator Scheme are
promising, but their effect on Western Cape arrivals is not yet proven.725 The province's task is to make the
case for reform in its own markets, with its own numbers.

The course of action
Wesgro runs a standing escalation agenda at Steerco. Each item names the decision, the national body
that owns it and the date the province asks for. The first agenda is tabled in 2027 (section 4.3). It covers
visa categories and processing for China, India, the Middle East and Africa. It also covers air service
agreements and immigration throughput at Cape Town International. Wesgro's Research and Destination
Marketing units build the evidence for each ask, market by market. Stakeholder insights already point to
the evidence needed: the safety issues most damaging to demand and the access barriers across air,
visas and onward travel.726

The province delivers its own share in parallel. The Western Cape Government, the municipalities and
SAPS set up the dedicated tourism safety units and programmes the Metro needs.727 Safety compacts
follow in pilot communities. Municipalities and CapeNature fix basic services, water and connectivity at
remote reserves and in tourism towns. Wesgro carries a visitor-safety narrative in its marketing and
applies the four launch gates to any community product it sells. From 2029 responses to the national asks
are logged and followed up each quarter.

Why it can work
Escalation works when the ask is specific and the evidence is local. A national department weighs many
claims; one that names the decision, the market and the arrivals at stake is easier to grant. A standing
agenda keeps each ask alive across budget cycles, so a refusal becomes a follow-up rather than an end.
It also gives coordination one anchor. Chile's coordination is most powerful when anchored around a
single strategic constraint, long-haul access.728 Action on its own share, from safety units to basic services,
strengthens the province's hand when it asks others to act.

Chile pairs national access policy with airport investment. It runs one of the world's most liberal aviation

lesson is to lobby actively for air service liberalisation and simplified visa regimes.732 Kenya reduces entry
friction to position Nairobi as a regional hub, and every comparator was scored on ease of access and
visas.733 No comparator shows a region escalating to its national government, so the province's own
record is the first real test. The condition is a national answer. The province cannot compel one, so it
measures what it can: visa processing times in priority markets and the share of its asks answered
(section 5.2).

THEME 3.2 · GOVERNANCE AND LEADERSHIP · FOCUS AREA 20


Develop workforce and service skills
The 300,000 tourism-linked jobs ambition has no baseline and no labour analysis behind it,
so the first step is to commission that analysis, and the skills plan follows from it.

The challenge
The province cannot yet count the jobs its strategy promises. The ambition of 300,000 tourism-linked jobs
is one of four headline ambitions, with no base year, baseline or target year. No labour analysis sits
behind it.734 The Department of Tourism reports that no approved methodology exists for provincial tourism
GDP and jobs estimates.735 This focus area therefore rests on analysis still to be commissioned. Section
2.4 sets out why a jobs baseline must come before a jobs target.

Jobs matter to the strategy as much as arrivals. Wesgro's mission is to drive inclusive and sustainable
growth and create jobs in Cape Town and the Western Cape.736 Service quality also rests on people.
Mossel Bay names inconsistent restaurant quality and service delivery as a major weakness.737
CapeNature is cautious about scaling international demand because service readiness is uneven across
its sites.738

Yet skills gaps surface only in passing. CapeNature finds low baseline skills in communities near reserves,
which need substantial upskilling before they can meet service standards.739 The West Coast District
Municipality reports stretched municipal officials, and the Overberg calls for substantial mentoring and
business support.740 Each is a statement, not a measure.

What the evidence shows

2025. Until the analysis reports, Chapter 5 carries jobs without a baseline or target. It sets the targets at
the first gate, at the end of 2028, so the baseline must be in place by then (sections 4.7 and 5.3).

The skills plan follows the analysis and runs through the provincial quality charter (focus area 11). The
Singapore lesson behind it pairs a province-wide quality framework with investment in workforce
capability.746 From 2029 CapeNature, CATHSSETA, training providers and municipalities run training
partnerships around reserves and in pilot communities. LTOs and tour operators help newly trained guides
stay in the sector with bookings from existing itineraries. Nationally, the province asks the Department of
Tourism to approve a provincial jobs methodology and Stats SA to resource the tourism satellite account.

3.4 The integrated case

The three pillars make one case. Demand work fills seats only if the seats exist, supply work
pays only if the product can be booked, and both need the enabling base to last.

The growth model sets out three paths from the 2025 base of 1,498,829 arrivals: 1,822,634 in 2035 on the
conservative path, 2,175,778 on the base path and 2,516,282 on the accelerated path.751 The deck is
explicit that the paths differ by intervention, not by demand potential.752 Each step of the growth case
therefore rests on a named set of interventions, and each set draws on focus areas in all three pillars.

The conservative path, 1,822,634 arrivals in 2035, holds existing air access with no added frequencies or
routes and continues trade and marketing, with growth in the mature markets and current corridors. Focus
areas 5 and 7 hold that base. The base path adds 353,144 arrivals to reach 2,175,778. It assumes
Emirates, Qatar, Ethiopian and Turkish Airlines add frequency and capacity and existing Wesgro
programmes expand, while India, China and regional Africa grow through hub connections. Focus areas 1
to 7, 9 and 10 deliver it. The accelerated path adds a further 340,504 to reach 2,516,282. It needs direct
India and China routes secured and dedicated activation in India, China, the USA and the GCC, with
India, China and the Middle East growing ahead of their source markets. Focus areas 4, 7, 16 and 19
deliver it on top of the base.753

The gateway binds first, at peak. The growth model draws current air access as a line at 1.8 million
inbound seats, and the base case crosses it at the 2030 year point.754 Relief at Cape Town International
arrives about 2032 for the international terminal and about 2033 for the runway.755 ACSA now schedules
the realigned runway for December 2029, subject to contract award. This plan keeps about 2033 as its
planning date and treats the earlier date as upside.756 Between those dates, growth has to come from
larger aircraft and added frequency on the full routes, from hub-carrier capacity and, for domestic
connections, from George, whose terminal doubles by 2028 or 2029 (focus areas 5, 6 and 7).

The deck draws arrivals and seats against one axis and prints no seat table behind the 1.8 million line. The
crossing year is read from the chart: after the 2028 point (1,673,855) and by the 2030 point (1,802,929).

If the supply moves slip, the case stops at the seat line. The deck states that the projected growth
cannot be realised without additional capacity or frequency,757 and that without confirmed routes the
accelerated case reverts to base.758 On the full routes, demand work cannot make up the gap, because
marketing raises fares rather than seats;759 on the hub routes, which have seats to spare, it can.

Below the gateway, the pillars depend on each other. Green-season demand needs seats in the
months carriers now cut (focus areas 2 and 7). Dispersal needs product that can be booked and reached
(focus areas 3, 9 and 10). Inclusion needs a seller and a safe visit before exposure (focus areas 13 and
19). Every target needs data that today sits with SA Tourism, ACSA and the booking platforms (focus area
18).

Air access and bookability move first, because every other lever runs through them. The first phase
works the seats and the booking path before the base case crosses the model's seat line, and the direct
routes wait for runway relief. Chapter 4 turns the sequence into eighteen initiatives with owners, phases
and decision gates.

Activate, 2027 to 2028, comes before the base case crosses the model's seat line around 2030. Seats
are held and added on the full routes (focus areas 5, 6 and 7), the green-season calendar is set (2) and
the bookability census and onboarding run (3). Two or three inclusion pilots start (13), Steerco, the data
protocol and the escalation agenda are set up (16, 18 and 19) and the labour analysis is commissioned
(20).

Expand, 2029 to 2031, brings hub conversion at scale (4), corridor product and mobility (9 and 10), the
quality charter (11) and flagship flow management (12), along with cruise product (8) and the investment
pipeline at financial close (15). The 2030 mid-term review tests the seat line, and terminal relief arrives
from about 2032.

Sustain, 2032 to 2035, takes direct India and China routes to carriers as runway capacity arrives (4 and
7), scales inclusion once its thresholds are met (13) and embeds climate risk and LTO funding (14).
Runway relief arrives about 2033, and the accelerated case depends on the direct routes. 760

Execution plan

4.1 From strategy to initiatives
Twenty focus areas become eighteen initiatives, one per focus area except where one
owner delivers two.

The plan follows one chain from vision to measure. Each level answers a different question. The pillar
says what must change and the focus area says where; the initiative says what Wesgro and its partners
do, and the indicator says how Steerco will know it has happened (section 5.1).

Seven levels run from the vision to the measures, and each has a fixed count in this plan. One vision says
where the province is going: grow international arrivals from 1.5m in 2025 toward 2.5m by 2035. Three
pillars say what must change, such as Reshape demand. Six themes say in which part of the system,
such as source market growth and diversification. Twenty focus areas say where the evidence says to act,
such as Convert India and China hub traffic. Eighteen initiatives say what Wesgro and its partners do,
such as I4 India and China hub conversion. The 112 actions say who does what, by tier, such as
activation inside Xiaohongshu, Douyin and MakeMyTrip. The indicators, 7 at impact, 25 at outcome and
36 at output level, say how Steerco knows, such as T2-06 India and China hub arrivals. The counts come
from Chapter 3, the action tables and the traceability table in Appendix A1.

Two merges take twenty focus areas to eighteen initiatives. Segment-led marketing and mature-market
yield share one owner and one set of channels, so focus areas 1 and 5 form initiative I1. Institutional
alignment and industry collaboration share one forum, so focus areas 16 and 17 form I15. Every other
focus area has its own initiative, numbered I1 to I18 in one sequence so that the M&E toolkit can track
them on one list.

Leads are proposed, not agreed. They follow the Wesgro units named in the Inception Report's
stakeholder map and the actions in Chapter 3, and Steerco confirms them at its first meeting [Steerco to
confirm initiative leads and partners]. Six initiatives have a partner as the accountable lead, because the
main lever sits outside Wesgro's mandate: corridor mobility, quality standards, flagship sites, community
pilots, LTO funding and skills.

  Eighteen initiatives cover the twenty focus areas, and eleven of them are in implementation by 2028.

4.2 Sequencing and phases

Three phases run to 2035, and the first is weighted to the moves that change the 2030
constraint: seats, bookability, green-season events and the inclusion pilots.

The phases follow calendar years: Activate from 2027 to 2028, Expand from 2029 to 2031 and Sustain
from 2032 to 2035. Each phase ends at a decision gate tied to the Chapter 5 indicators (section 4.7). The
first gate, at the end of 2028, falls in Wesgro's FY2028/29, two years before the base case crosses the
model's seat line around 2030.761




Figure 4.1. Initiative timeline, 2027 to 2035
Source: Letsema, initiative portfolio (sections 4.1 and 4.3). Design means scoping, data, agreements and pilots; implement means delivery at
scale.


Phase 1: Activate, 2027 to 2028
Phase 1 works inside Wesgro's mandate and budget and on the levers that move before the airport can. It
holds and adds seats on the full routes and builds the air access fund (I6). It publishes the green-season
calendar (I2), counts and starts to close the bookability gap (I3) and starts two or three inclusion pilots
(I12). Steerco, the data-sharing protocol and the escalation agenda start in the first quarter (I15, I16, I17),
because every other initiative reports through them.

The marketing initiatives (I1, I4, I5) and cruise (I7) spend 2027 on design: segment targets, hub-carrier
agreements, the Nigeria and Kenya plans and excursion products. Seven initiatives use both years for
design (I8 to I11, I13, I14, I18). Their first task is a baseline or a case that no deliverable holds: corridor
761
      Demand-Side Analysis, Jun 2026, p21.


Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory                                                                                     135
Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                       DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



mobility, product, quality, flagship capacity, climate risk and LTO funding, the investment pipeline and the
labour analysis. The gate at the end of 2028 asks three questions. Have seats on the full routes held?
Does the bookability baseline exist and is it moving? Has any pilot met its thresholds?

Phase 2: Expand, 2029 to 2031
Phase 2 scales what Phase 1 proved and meets the model's seat line. Hub conversion, regional Africa
and segment marketing run at scale. Corridor mobility and product move from design to delivery, on the
Garden Route, the Winelands and the Overberg first. The quality charter is adopted and phased by
district, and the investment pipeline reaches financial close on its first projects. The mid-term review in
2030 tests arrivals against the base path when the base case crosses the model's seat line, and checks
that the runway is on its published dates (section 5.6).

Phase 3: Sustain, 2032 to 2035
Phase 3 runs as relief arrives at Cape Town International: the international terminal about 2032 and the
runway about 2033.762 The direct India and China route cases built since 2027 go to carriers as capacity
frees up, which is the step the accelerated case depends on. Inclusion pilots that met their thresholds
scale through channels, climate risk and LTO funding are embedded in municipal planning, and the
terminal evaluation in 2035 closes the plan (section 5.6).




762
      Infrastructure Report, Jul 2026, p103.

Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                     DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION




4.3 Initiative charters

Each initiative has a one-page charter that a lead can run from and Steerco can hold to
account.

Every charter uses the same fields. It states the objective, the focus areas served, the proposed lead and
partners and the tier of government. It then sets the actions in each phase, marked Design or Implement,
and closes with the dependencies, the funding route, the risks and the indicators it moves. Actions come
from the Chapter 3 action tables. Leads are proposals for Steerco to confirm, and rand bands are set in
section 4.6.

4.4 Roles and accountabilities

Wesgro is accountable for twelve initiatives and does part of the work in all eighteen; for the
other six, the accountable lead sits with another tier, and the plan names it.

The matrix below assigns each initiative to the four roles: responsible (R) for doing the work, accountable
(A) for the result, consulted (C) before decisions and informed (I) of progress. Where Wesgro is
accountable and does the work itself, the cell reads A/R. Assignments are proposals for Steerco to
confirm, built from the actions in Chapter 3.

Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                         DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



National government and the state-owned entities
Four levers sit outside the province and cannot be bought or marketed into place. Route rights and visas
sit with the national tier,763 the airport and port capital programmes with national SOEs764 and policing with
SAPS. The plan treats them as an escalation agenda (I17), carried by Steerco through DEDAT to the
national departments and SOEs. Each item is stated as a decision, an owner and a date the province is
asking for. Wesgro's own route work (I6) runs inside the existing agreements meanwhile.

Provincial government
DEDAT is a standing member of Steerco and the accountable lead where the lever is policy or funding
rather than promotion: quality standards (I10), LTO funding and climate risk (I13) and the labour analysis
(I18). The Department of Mobility leads the transport half of corridor mobility (I8). Wesgro's units lead the
demand, access, product and data initiatives and report through the programme office.

District and local government
Municipalities and LTOs hold the assets, the selling bodies and the visitor services that the regional and
inclusion initiatives depend on. They lead the community selling bodies in the pilots (I12), attach
covenants to public assets (I12, I14) and carry the route and product work in their districts (I3, I9). The City
of Cape Town and Cape Town Tourism are partners on events, mobility and data (I2, I8, I16) and sit on
Steerco.

The private sector and site operators
Airlines decide frequency and gauge, hotel investors decide the mid-scale tier and attraction operators
manage their own capacity. The plan gives them the evidence and the demand case. It asks for the
decisions it cannot make for them: seats on full routes (I6), investment in secondary nodes (I14) and
visitor-flow management at flagship sites, where the operators are accountable (I11).

4.6 Funding and investment

Most of the plan runs on budgets that already exist; the capital it needs sits with ACSA,
PRASA, Transnet and private investors, and none of it has been costed yet.

No deliverable costs any initiative. The Inception Report scoped an investment opportunity framework with
return scoring, but no separate output was delivered,765 and stakeholders describe Western Cape tourism
funding as materially below peer destinations without giving a figure.766 Thirteen initiatives draw mainly on
Wesgro's operating budget, and four need private capital as a main source; the initiatives table in section
4.1 gives each one's main and supporting funding routes. This section leaves the rand bands to be set
with Wesgro Finance and DEDAT [cost band per initiative and phase: Wesgro Finance and DEDAT to set
before Steerco signs off the plan].




Figure 4.2. Initiatives by main funding source
Source: Letsema, count of initiatives by main funding source from Table 4.1. An initiative can have more than one main source.

Crowding in capital. The rule for capital is the Inclusion Plan's: invest after demand is shown, and attach
a maintenance and marketing covenant to any public asset.767 Wesgro's investment promotion work builds
the tourism pipeline from the Infrastructure Report's gap-closure tables, where every district lists tourism
gaps with no committed capital (I14).768 The air access fund scales Cape Town Air Access's existing route
fund with public and private money (I6).

What the SLA asks and what is still to do. The SLA asks for return projections on infrastructure
priorities (clause 1.1.8.3) [clause wording to be checked against the signed Annexure B]. Those need
costed projects, and the only costed items in hand are the airport, rail and port programmes the province
does not fund. The return case is therefore an output of I14 in Phase 1, built project by project as the
pipeline forms.

4.7 Delivery governance

One Steerco, one programme office and four fixed decision points keep eighteen initiatives
and three tiers of government on one plan.




Figure 4.3. Delivery governance structure
Source: Letsema, proposed delivery governance.

Steerco. A Tourism Growth Steerco co-chaired by Wesgro's CEO and DEDAT's Head of Department,
with Cape Town Tourism as a standing member and the City of Cape Town, the district municipalities and
the industry bodies at the table. It is distinct from the Unlocking Tourism Steerco that oversees this study.
It receives the quarterly scorecard, clears cross-initiative issues, holds the escalation agenda and
recommends gate decisions to the Board. Stakeholders asked for this kind of alignment directly: mobility
responsibilities alone are split across three tiers of government.769

Programme office. A small office inside Wesgro runs the plan on a build, operate and transfer basis. It
builds the charters, the dashboard and the reporting calendar in Phase 1, runs them through Phase 2 and
hands them to the line units by the end of 2031. It owns the scorecard and the dashboard, and Wesgro
Research owns the indicator definitions and the data protocol (I16).

Cadence. Initiative leads report monthly to the programme office, Steerco meets quarterly and the Board
receives an annual outlook, which also anchors the Unlocking Tourism business outlook event. The plan
is refreshed each year against the indicators and the gate decisions.

Table 4.4. Decision gates and reviews

      Four decision points test the plan against its indicators, and each one decides what the next phase funds.

4.8 Risks and dependencies

Ten of the fourteen material risks sit outside Wesgro's control, so the mitigation for most of
them is early warning and a route to the tier that can act.

5.1 Results chain
Every initiative is measured at three levels: what it produces, what changes because of it
and what that adds to the 2035 case.

The framework is the written form of the M&E toolkit the SLA requires (clauses 1.1.4 and 1.1.10). It sets
indicators, baselines and targets for arrivals, spend, jobs, airlift, cruise and SMME inclusion, with the tools
to track them [clause wording to be checked against the signed Annexure B]. The Inception Report adds
geographic spread and asks for definitions, sources, frequency, owners and governance for each
indicator.770




Figure 5.1. Results chain for the plan
Source: Letsema, results chain for the plan.

The table below gives the chain for each initiative, pillar by pillar. Tier 3 output indicators come from the
initiative charters, Tier 2 outcome indicators from the focus areas and Tier 1 impact indicators from the
growth case.

      initiatives (I12 to I18) move community spend and jobs, and supply the governance and data every other

5.3 Baselines and targets

The arrivals targets come straight from the growth model, while the jobs and other Tier 1
targets wait for baselines that do not yet exist.

Baselines are fixed at 2025, the growth model's base year, for the growth-model indicators and at
FY2024/25 for the airport series, the recovery baseline used in Chapter 1. Targets follow two paths: the
base case, which assumes Wesgro's current activity continues with hub carriers adding capacity, and the
accelerated case, which adds direct India and China routes and dedicated activation.771 The model prints
the base case for 2028, 2030, 2032, 2034 and 2035, and the other paths for 2035 only. The 2031 base
value and the accelerated milestones are computed and marked as such.




Figure 5.2. Arrivals paths and milestones to 2035
Source: Market Assessment Report, May 2026, p19 (2025 base); Demand-Side Analysis, Jun 2026, p20, p21 (2028 and 2035 points; 1.8m
seat line). The 2031 base value is the geometric mean of the printed 2030 and 2032 points; the accelerated and conservative milestones are
computed at a constant rate between the printed 2025 and 2035 values.

Arrivals. The model's 2025 base and the airport series measure different things: arrivals on the growth
model's basis, which Skift is to confirm (section 1.1), and international passenger arrivals at Cape Town
International. The airport series is the quarterly proxy because ACSA publishes it, and it stood at 1.44m in
FY24/25 and 1.57m in FY25/26.772 Both are reported, each against its own history.

Spend. T1-03's path holds spend per arrival fixed, so its targets are in constant 2024 rand, while SA
Tourism reports nominal rand; progress is read after deflating by CPI. The model's 2024 spend of
R27.12bn sits 19% above the R22.7bn SA Tourism reported for FY2024.773 [Open item: Skift to rebase the
spend path on the FY2025 actual of R25.9bn.]

Jobs. No deliverable carries a provincial tourism employment baseline, and the Pero tourism table could
not be retrieved [provincial tourism employment baseline: Wesgro Research to supply from the Pero 2025
or the Stats SA provincial TSA]. T1-04 therefore carries no baseline or modelled path until the labour
analysis sets a provincial measure (I18).

The report restates the 300,000 as a total-sector figure for the labour analysis to test (I18). For context,
public sources put direct tourism employment at 106,707 in Cape Town in 2024774 and 953,981 nationally
in 2024.775

Green season. The 2025 month table sums to 104.9%. Normalised, it gives 61.9% for October to March
and 38.1% for the green season, in line with the printed 61% and 39% for 2024.776 T1-05 uses 38.1% as
its baseline.

Spread and community spend. No deliverable prints district visitor spend, so the share outside the
Metro has no baseline until the data protocol delivers one (I16). The community share starts from the
Inclusion Plan's traced floor; its 1% is a reference point the plan prints, not a target any party has
adopted.777

5.4 Data sources and gaps

Arrivals, seats and cruise volumes can be tracked from existing feeds; spread, bookability,
jobs and community spend cannot until new data is built or shared.

Stakeholders put data among the top barriers: no approved methodology exists for provincial tourism GDP
and jobs,778 and no one holds a single view of visitor behaviour.779 The data-sharing protocol under focus
area 18 (I16) is the route to closing the gaps below.

5.5 Tracking mechanism

A quarterly scorecard to Steerco, an annual outlook to the Board and a live dashboard
where the sources allow it.

Four principles run the tracking. Automate before collecting by hand: arrivals, seats and cruise volumes
come from feeds that already exist. Name one owner for the dashboard (the programme office) and one
for the definitions (Wesgro Research). Report every indicator against its own baseline and both paths.
Refresh the register each year at the plan review.




Figure 5.3. Quarterly scorecard layout
Source: Letsema, dashboard concept. Illustrative layout; no values shown.

Reporting runs on a fixed calendar tied to Steerco and the Board. Each month, the leads send Tier 3
outputs, initiative status and the seats and arrivals feeds to the programme office as a dashboard update.
Each quarter, the programme office takes Tier 2 outcomes where available, the risks rated high and the
escalation responses to Steerco in the scorecard. Each year, Steerco takes Tier 1 impact against both
paths, the plan refresh and gate status to the Board in an outlook report, made public at the business
outlook event. At each gate, Steerco sends the Board a gate paper on the tests in section 4.7.




Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory                                                    174
Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                        DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION




5.6 Evaluation and learning

Two evaluations bracket the plan: a mid-term review in 2030, when the base case crosses
the model's seat line, and a terminal evaluation in 2035.

The mid-term review tests whether the plan is on its path and whether the path still holds. It falls in 2030
because that is when the base case crosses the model's 1.8 million seat line,780 before runway relief on
the planning date.781 The terminal evaluation in 2035 measures the result against both paths and sets up
the next plan. Both should be run by a party independent of the programme office.

Each pillar carries two evaluation questions, one on results and one on the assumptions behind them. For
Reshape demand: did arrivals, spend per visitor and the green-season share move with the activation,
and in which markets and segments? Is conversion still the constraint, or has access overtaken it in the
growth markets? For Transform supply: did seats, readiness and bookability rise outside the Metro, and
did visitors follow? Did the airport relief arrive on time, and did the second gateways carry the load
meanwhile? For Build enabling capability: did the pilots meet their thresholds, and did community spend
rise? Did the national asks get answers, and did the data protocol deliver the baselines?

Pilots before capital. The inclusion pilots are evaluated before any capital is committed, on the Inclusion
Plan's own measures: bookings, visitors and locally retained revenue.782 A pilot scales only when it meets
the thresholds agreed before launch.783 The same rule applies to any product or asset investment under I9
and I14: test demand, then invest.

Learning into the plan. Findings from each review, gate and pilot go into the annual plan refresh under
section 4.7, so the charters, targets and sequence change with the evidence rather than at the end of the
plan.


5.7 Toolkit handover

The M&E toolkit turns this chapter into four working files that Wesgro Research owns from
handover.

The four files are the indicator register, the baseline workbook, the scorecard template and the dashboard
data model (SLA clauses 1.1.10.2 and 1.1.15.3) [clause wording to be checked against the signed
Annexure B]. The indicator register holds all 68 indicators with their definition, unit, source, frequency,
owner, baseline and target by year and path. The baseline workbook holds the baseline values with page
references to the deliverables, and the computed values with their method. Wesgro Research owns both.
The programme office owns the scorecard template, which carries the quarterly Steerco scorecard and
the annual Board outlook. It also owns the dashboard data model: the feeds, refresh frequency and
manual-entry forms for the dashboard in section 5.5.

Handover runs through knowledge-transfer sessions with Wesgro Research and the programme office, as
the SLA requires (clause 1.1.15). The Excel toolkit itself is outside this report and is listed as an open item
[M&E toolkit workbook: Letsema to deliver with the final report].

A2 Demand model

The demand model takes international arrivals from 1,498,829 in 2025 to 1,822,634,
2,175,778 or 2,516,282 in 2035, and its base case crosses the 1.8 million seat line the
model draws, at the 2030 year point.

Method
The model sets a growth rate for each source market from four levers and projects arrivals from the base
year to 2035.784 The levers are source market growth, Western Cape penetration of source-market
outbound, an airlift capacity ceiling and structural market conditions. Source-market growth sets the
ceiling: "WC cannot sustainably grow faster than it's source market is generating travellers". 785 Projections
are anchored on OAG aviation data and Skift global traveller data.786

Scenario definitions
The three scenarios vary intervention, not demand: "The difference between scenarios is not demand
potential, but the extent to which structural barriers are removed and growth opportunities are actively
pursued".787

Table A.3. Growth model scenario definitions

      The scenarios step up from no new airlift or growth-market spend to direct India and China routes with

Seat line and model limits
Both decks state that "Current air access places a hard ceiling at 1.8 million inbound seats, meaning the
projected growth cannot be realised without additional capacity or frequency".795 Neither deck prints the
seats behind the line or a load factor that converts seats into arrivals. At the 79% average load factor, 1.8
million seats would carry about 1.42 million arrivals, below the 1.57 million recorded in FY25/26, so this
report reads the line as the model's assumption, not a measured cap (section 1.3).

The model prints its seat line and six base-case year points but no seat table, annual series or group split
for the other scenarios. The seat line is 1.8 million inbound seats, drawn as a line labelled 1.8M and called

      Demand-Side Analysis, Jun 2026, p27.

      Market Assessment Report, May 2026, p10, p24.

      Market Assessment Report, May 2026, p20; Demand-Side Analysis, Jun 2026, p21.


Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory                                                                                 183
                                                                                                         DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



current, with no year or seat definition.796 Neither deck states the year the line is crossed: 2028
(1,673,855) sits below the line and 2030 (1,802,929) is the first point above it. 797

No seat table is printed: there are no seats by route or year, no load factors and no frequencies. 798 Nor is
an annual series: 2026, 2027, 2029, 2031 and 2033 are absent, and the June chart starts at 2024, not
2025.799 The conservative and accelerated scenarios appear at 2035 only, with no group split, no year
points and no per-market rates.800

The calendar or financial basis and the source series of the 1,498,829 base are not stated, and the 2025
bars are not marked as actuals or model values.801 ACSA passenger arrivals (1.57m in FY25/26), SAT
tourist arrivals, OAG air arrivals and model arrivals are not reconciled.802

Arrivals figures and their bases
Eight arrivals figures circulate around this strategy, and they count four things: ambitions, airport
passengers, South African Tourism's tourist arrivals and the model's arrivals. The table below fixes each
figure and states how the report uses it. It keeps an airport count apart from a provincial tourist count, and
an ambition apart from a projection. Section 1.1 sets out the three rules behind the last column.

Table A.9. Arrivals figures in circulation

      The report uses airport passenger arrivals for baselines, the demand model for targets and South African
      Tourism's provincial count only to read across to the Growth for Jobs target.

 Figure                  What it counts                       Year        Source                How this report uses it
 1.44m                   International passenger arrivals     FY24/25     Current State         Recovery baseline (1.2) and

Annual 2025; public source.

The two ambitions do not reconcile with each other or with the measured baseline. Neither the 2.8 million
ambition nor its later restatement as 5.7 million arrivals and R54.7 billion of spend names a series or a
base year.803 Twice the 1.44 million international passenger arrivals at Cape Town International in
FY24/25 is 2.88 million, close to the first and far from the second.804

The model's 2025 base of 1,498,829 sits just below South African Tourism's count of 1,538,142 for
2025.805 Yet each market group's 2025 value tracks its 2024 value grown at the model's own rate. The
report therefore treats the base as a model value until its basis is confirmed.806

The airport count for FY25/26, 1.57 million, sits above the model's 2025 base and below its 2028 base-
case point of 1,673,855.807 Yet the two differ in measure and period, so the gap is no evidence that the
model is cautious.

A3 Benchmark cases

Six comparators were drawn from a 30-destination screen on twelve criteria, and each case
reduces to a system type, two levers and the changes they require of the Western Cape.

Evaluation criteria
Both the Inception Report and the Global Benchmarking Report screen destinations on twelve criteria in
four groups.808 Each destination received one average score across all criteria.809

are shown as points and Valencia's second lever takes its p49 title in place of the p9 label. Melbourne's ~4.3% is a forecast average annual
growth rate for short-term visitor arrivals to Australia, not a 2023 to 2024 actual (p17). New Zealand's ~12% matches holiday-arrivals growth

product yield optimisation raises value per traveller without increasing arrivals. Corrected 2024 figures: Chile 40.4% (116% of 2019),
Singapore 21% (86% of 2019), Kenya 14.6% (section 2.1).


System type and its three components
The report treats sustained growth as the product of three reinforcing components: institutional
coordination; strategic marketing and promotion; accessibility and connectivity.818 Without competitive
access, "latent demand does not convert".819

All six cases align coordination, marketing and access, but each builds its system around a different
anchor.820

Melbourne and Victoria: events-led. Coordination institutionalises events and a knowledge-led brand as
a cross-government economic development tool. Marketing integrates city branding, intellectual capital
and events into a single demand proposition. The event calendar is used to de-risk and stimulate aviation
demand.

Chile: connectivity-led. Coordination is anchored around a single strategic constraint, long-haul access.
Marketing runs unified national branding with defined regional identities and multi-region itinerary
packaging. Liberal aviation policy and major hub investment position Santiago as a single efficient long-
haul hub.

Kenya: product-led. Coordination aligns tourism with national priorities in conservation, trade and
diplomacy. Marketing is segment-specific and grounded in a designed product system. Access reduces
entry friction, with Nairobi as a multipurpose regional and global hub.

Singapore: orchestrated experience. A centrally orchestrated system under STB works through
structured public-private partnerships. Marketing packages experiences (events, precincts, lifestyle) into
seamless high-value propositions. Aviation, infrastructure and visitor experience are integrated into a
single seamless gateway.



818
      Global Benchmarking Report, Mar 2026, p58.
819
      Global Benchmarking Report, Mar 2026, p59.
820
      Global Benchmarking Report, Mar 2026, p61 for the system types; p59 to p60 for the three components, condensed.


Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory                                                                                     188
Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                                         DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



Valencia: governance-led. Governance is decentralised and sector-led. Marketing expands the reasons
to visit: gastronomy, culture, sport, wellness and business events. Connectivity supports a multi-product
destination system and dispersal.

New Zealand: value-led. Tourism, aviation and policy are integrated around shared demand objectives.
Marketing shifts from volume to value by selling scarce, high-quality experiences. Long-haul demand
converts only when marketing, aviation and distribution are aligned.

A4 Readiness scores

The infrastructure assessment classifies 50,332 assets in six districts and scores each
district on attractiveness, readiness and accessibility; overall ratings fall from 4.5 in the Cape
Metro to 1.9 in the Central Karoo.

Method
The assessment models tourism supply in three layers (attractions, services and infrastructure) and holds
that "destination performance is constrained by the weakest layer".822 Its asset database sorts the layers
into twelve categories with named subtypes.823 It draws on Wesgro, SANParks, StatsSA and CapeNature
data,824 with Overpass, WikiQuery and tourism reports for 2023 to 2025 named as sources of geographical
data points.825

Table A.14. Asset database structure

 Layer                    Category                        Subtypes
 Attraction               Natural                         Beach; mountain; park & reserve; scenic routes
 Attraction               Cultural & heritage             Museum; historical site; monument; cultural precinct
 Attraction               Commercial and leisure          Wine estate; theme park
 Attraction               Events                          Festival; sporting event; cultural event; business events
 Service                  Accommodation                   Hotel; guest house; lodge; self catering
 Service                  Food & beverage                 Restaurant; café; bar
 Service                  Tourism services                Tour operator; travel agency; car rental
 Service                  Visitor amenities               Fuel station; retail centre; visitor information centres
 Infrastructure           Air access                      Airport; airstrip
 Infrastructure           Road network                    National route; regional road
 Infrastructure           Transport nodes                 Bus terminal; train station
 Infrastructure           Public services                 Hospital; police station
Source: Infrastructure Report, Jul 2026, p10, as printed in sentence case.

The comparative tables score a different set of eleven categories in three groups,826 and the district
scorecards chart a similar set. Attractions cover activity, commercial & leisure, cultural & heritage, events
and natural assets. Services cover accommodation, eatery and shopping. Transport and enabling
structures cover transport, enabling structure and visitor amenities. The asset counts use five groups:
accommodation, attractions, eateries, events and amenities.827

Attractiveness carries 40% of the stated weight and readiness and accessibility carry 30% each.828
Attractiveness and quality asks how compelling the tourism offer is, through visitor ratings, uniqueness,
price positioning and experience depth. Readiness and capacity asks whether the region can service and
convert demand, through capacity and digital discovery. Accessibility asks whether visitors can reach and
move through the destination, through airport proximity, gateway travel time and public transport
availability. The scale runs from 1 'Very weak' and 2 'Weak' through 3 'Functional' and 4 'Strong' to 5

'Leading'. The stated method is: "Average indicator scores within each dimension, then combine
dimensions using the agreed weights".829

Each dimension is the average of its indicator scores, and the dimensions are then combined at
40/30/30.830 All three use a common scale from 1 to 5, with Functional at 3 and Leading at 5. 831 The report
states four assumptions.832 Asset volume does not equal readiness, and one framework applies to all
regions. Indicator scores are evidence informed rather than purely formula derived, and missing data is
not treated as poor performance.

Overall rating. The overall ratings are the Infrastructure Report's evidence-informed judgement, not a weighted
formula, so this report does not re-derive them.

accessibility score is constant within each group, except West Coast natural attractions at 2.0 against

enabling structures.

A5 Inclusion fact base

The Inclusion Plan scores 107 long-listed communities down to a shortlist of 20, rates their
readiness, sizes the spend they could capture and takes its order of action from five
benchmark cases.

Long-list rule and scoring weights
The long-list takes every Census 2011 sub-place cluster with 1,500 people or more and official
unemployment of 25% or more, plus named mission, fishing and farm settlements.841 The rule yields 107
township and rural communities with 2.81 million people in 2011 (48% of the province) and 3.79 million on
the 2025 estimate.842 Of the 107, 93 sit within 45 minutes of one of 35 supply-report nodes or three
corridors.843

By region the long-list holds Cape Metro 26, Cape Winelands 16, Overberg 19, Garden Route 23, West
Coast 13 and Central Karoo 10.844 The shortlist takes five from Cape Metro and three from each other
region.845 Need rests on Census 2011 because Census 2022 is not published below municipality and ward
and asked no income question.846

p17).


Archetypes and responses
Two tests set the archetype: a 45-minute drive time to the nearest node or corridor and whether any
attraction sits inside the boundary.847 Atlantis is held remote by Wesgro naming at 26 minutes from
Darling.848 For the largest group the plan's diagnosis is that "the product and the node's demand are there;
the seller is missing".849

Spend capture scenarios
The 20 communities hold 18.4% of Western Cape residents but a documented floor of R4m to R10m a
year in visitor spend, while 1% of FY2025 foreign direct spend would land R259m.859 They also hold
15.4% of employed residents; both shares come from Census 2011. Foreign direct spend traced to
businesses trading inside the communities is a floor of 0.02% to 0.04%, which the plan documents as
R4m to R10m landing in the 20 communities a year.
